L_{X}=d\iota(X)+\iota(X)d
for
X\in{\mathfrak{h}}
, we actually have a representation
{\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{h}}\curvearrowright H^{*}({\mathfrak{h}};V)
.

Proposition 21

(Hochschild–Serre
[
10
]
)
.

If
{\mathfrak{h}}
is an ideal of
{\mathfrak{g}}
, we have

E^{p,q}_{2}\simeq H^{p}\left({\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{h}};H^{q}({\mathfrak{h}};V)\right).

Proof.

See Corollary to Theorem 4 in
[
10
]
.
∎

6.2
A general consideration

Let
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{H}}
be a complex representation and
{\mathfrak{an}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\xi}}{{\curvearrowright}}V
be a real or complex representation such that
\xi({\mathfrak{n}})=0
. The Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence gives

E_{2}^{p,q}\simeq H^{p}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\right)\Rightarrow H^{p+q}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V).

The tensor product
{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V
is taken over
{\mathbb{C}}
when
V
is complex. The
E_{2}
sheet is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{1}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{1}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{0}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{0}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\right).}

Since there are no nontrivial differentials at
E_{2}
, the spectral sequence degenerates at
E_{2}
and therefore
E_{2}\simeq E_{\infty}
. Hence we get

\displaystyle H^{0}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)

\displaystyle\simeq H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{0}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\right),

(11)

\displaystyle H^{1}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)

\displaystyle\simeq H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{1}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\right)\oplus H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{0}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\right),

(12)

\displaystyle H^{2}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)

\displaystyle\simeq H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{1}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\right).

(13)

There exists an exact sequence

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\to H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\right)

\displaystyle\to H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L_{H}}}{{\to}}H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)

\displaystyle\to H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\right)\to 0

for
q=0,1
. So we need to compute the kernel and cokernel of the map

H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L_{H}}}{{\to}}H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)

(14)

to compute the cohomology
H^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)
. We have an isomorphism

\left(C^{*}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V),d\right)\simeq\left(C^{*}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}})\otimes V,d\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)

(15)

of cochain complexes since
\xi({\mathfrak{n}})=0
. Therefore

H^{*}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\simeq H^{*}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}})\otimes V.

We have

L_{H}=L_{H}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(H)

through the isomorphism (
15
), hence on the cohomologies. Assume that
\xi(H)
is a scalar multiplication. The map (
14
) becomes

\left(L_{H}+\xi(H)\right)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\colon H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}})\otimes V\to H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}})\otimes V.

Hence

\displaystyle H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\ker\left(H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L_{H}+\xi(H)}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}})\right)\otimes V,

(16)

\displaystyle H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}\nolimits\left(H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L_{H}+\xi(H)}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}})\right)\otimes V.

(17)

So it suffices to compute the kernel and cokernel of the map

L_{H}+\xi(H)\colon H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}})\to H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}})

to compute the
E_{2}
of the spectral sequence.

When
H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}})
is finite dimensional, the kernel and cokernel of
L_{H}+\xi(H)
on
H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}})
have the same dimension. Therefore
H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\right)
and
H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\right)
are isomorphic, but not canonically.

6.3
The
{\mathfrak{n}}
-invariant distributions of irreducible unitary representations

Let
\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{H}}
be an irreducible unitary representation,
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
be its derivative representation on the space of
C^{\infty}
vectors and
{\mathfrak{an}}\curvearrowright{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}
for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
be the representation defined in Section
4
. We will compute
H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)
. By (
16
) and (
17
) we need to compute the kernel and cokernel of

L_{H}+\lambda\colon H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}))\to H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})).

So we need to compute the
{\mathfrak{an}}
-module
H^{*}({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}))
.

First we have

\displaystyle H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)

\displaystyle=C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})^{\pi({\mathfrak{n}})}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}_{0}&{\mathcal{H}}={\mathbb{C}}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(18)

This can be proved by using a realization of the representation
{\mathcal{H}}
.

To compute
H^{1}({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}))
we use the following computation of the
{\mathfrak{n}}
-invariant distributions.

Proposition 22

(Flaminio–Forni
[
6
]
)
.

We have

\left(C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})/\pi({\mathfrak{n}})C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)^{*}\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}_{0}&{\mathcal{H}}={\mathbb{C}}\\
{\mathbb{C}}_{-\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}}&{\mathcal{H}}=D_{n}^{\pm}\\
{\mathbb{C}}_{-\frac{1+\sqrt{1+8\mu}}{2\sqrt{2}}}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}_{-\frac{1-\sqrt{1+8\mu}}{2\sqrt{2}}}&{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{\mu}\quad\left(\mu\neq-\frac{1}{8}\right)\\
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{\sharp}&{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{-\frac{1}{8}}\end{cases}

as
{\mathfrak{an}}
-modules, where the left hand side denotes the space of all linear maps from
C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})/\pi({\mathfrak{n}})C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
to
{\mathbb{C}}
, and
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{\sharp}={\mathbb{C}}^{2}
is a two dimensional representation of
{\mathfrak{an}}
defined by

H\mapsto-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
1&1\end{pmatrix},\quad E\mapsto 0.

Remark 23
.

Equip
C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
with the
C^{\infty}
topology. Consider the space

{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{H}})=\left\{\varphi\colon C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\to{\mathbb{C}}\ \middle|\ \text{$\varphi$ is linear and continuous}\right\}

of distributions and the space

{\mathcal{D}}_{E}({\mathcal{H}})=\left\{\varphi\in{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{H}})\ \middle|\ E\varphi=0\right\}

of
E
-invariant distributions, where
E\varphi\in{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{H}})
is defined by
(E\varphi)(f)=-\varphi\left(\pi(E)f\right)
for
f\in C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
. Then we have

{\mathcal{D}}_{E}({\mathcal{H}})\simeq\left(C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})/\pi({\mathfrak{n}})C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)^{*}

since the computation in Section 3.1 and 3.2 of Flaminio–Forni
[
6
]
shows any linear
\varphi\colon C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
such that
E\varphi=0
is automatically continuous.

It follows that

\displaystyle H_{0}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)

\displaystyle=C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})/\pi({\mathfrak{n}})C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})

\displaystyle\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}_{0}&{\mathcal{H}}={\mathbb{C}}\\
{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}}&{\mathcal{H}}=D_{n}^{\pm}\\
{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1+\sqrt{1+8\mu}}{2\sqrt{2}}}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1-\sqrt{1+8\mu}}{2\sqrt{2}}}&{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{\mu}\quad\left(\mu\neq-\frac{1}{8}\right)\\
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{\flat}&{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{-\frac{1}{8}},\end{cases}

where
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{\flat}={\mathbb{C}}^{2}
is a two dimensional representation of
{\mathfrak{an}}
defined by

H\mapsto\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
&1\end{pmatrix},\quad E\mapsto 0.

To go from homology to cohomology, we use the following isomorphism, which is a special case of a more general isomorphism.

Lemma 24
.

We have

{\mathfrak{n}}\otimes H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)\simeq H_{0}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)

as
{\mathfrak{an}}
-modules.

Proof.

Recall that the Chevalley–Eilenberg complex for Lie algebra homology is defined by

C_{*}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)=\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{n}}\otimes C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})

and

\displaystyle\partial\left(X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{p}\otimes f\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{p}(-1)^{i+1}X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{X_{i}}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{p}\otimes X_{i}f

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{i<j}(-1)^{i+j}[X_{i},X_{j}]\wedge X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{X_{i}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{X_{j}}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{p}\otimes f.

Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{n}}\otimes C^{1}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{n}}\otimes C^{0}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

where the upper horizontal arrow is defined by

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{n}}\otimes{\mathfrak{n}}^{*}\otimes C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})

\displaystyle X\otimes\varphi\otimes f

\displaystyle\mapsto\varphi(X)f

and the lower horizontal arrow is

\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\colon{\mathfrak{n}}\otimes C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\to{\mathfrak{n}}\otimes C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}).

Then this is commutative and all maps are
{\mathfrak{an}}
-equivariant. Hence we get the isomorphism of the lemma.
∎

Using this we get isomorphisms

\displaystyle H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)

\displaystyle\simeq H_{0}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)\otimes{\mathfrak{n}}^{*}

\displaystyle\simeq H_{0}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)\otimes{\mathbb{R}}_{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}

\displaystyle\simeq H_{0}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}

of
{\mathfrak{an}}
-modules. Therefore

H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}_{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}&{\mathcal{H}}={\mathbb{C}}\\
{\mathbb{C}}_{-\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}}&{\mathcal{H}}=D_{n}^{\pm}\\
{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{-1+\sqrt{1+8\mu}}{2\sqrt{2}}}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{-1-\sqrt{1+8\mu}}{2\sqrt{2}}}&{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{\mu}\quad\left(\mu\neq-\frac{1}{8}\right)\\
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{\natural}&{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{-\frac{1}{8}},\end{cases}

where
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{\natural}={\mathbb{C}}^{2}
is a two dimensional representation of
{\mathfrak{an}}
defined by

H\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}&\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\\
&-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\end{pmatrix},\quad E\mapsto 0.

By (
16
) and (
17
), we have

\displaystyle H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\ker\left(H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L_{H}+\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}))\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda},

\displaystyle H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}\nolimits\left(H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L_{H}+\lambda}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{q}({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}))\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}.

Hence the
E_{2}
of the spectral sequence is computed as follows.

Lemma 25
.

\displaystyle H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\begin{cases}0&\lambda\neq 0\\
{\mathbb{C}}&\lambda=0\end{cases}

H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)\right)\simeq H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)\right)=0

for a nontrivial irreducible unitary representation
{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathbb{C}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\begin{cases}0&\lambda\neq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\\
{\mathbb{C}}&\lambda=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\begin{cases}0&\lambda\neq\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}\\
{\mathbb{C}}&\lambda=\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mu})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mu})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\begin{cases}0&\lambda\neq\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1+8\mu}}{2\sqrt{2}}\\
{\mathbb{C}}&\lambda=\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1+8\mu}}{2\sqrt{2}}\end{cases}

for
\mu\neq-\frac{1}{8}

\displaystyle H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}\left({\mathcal{H}}_{-\frac{1}{8}}\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}};H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{n}};C^{\infty}\left({\mathcal{H}}_{-\frac{1}{8}}\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\begin{cases}0&\lambda\neq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\\
{\mathbb{C}}&\lambda=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\end{cases}

6.4
Computation results of
H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)
for an irreducible unitary representation
{\mathcal{H}}

By Lemma
25
and the isomorphisms (
11
), (
12
), (
13
), we get the following computation results of
H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)
for an irreducible unitary representation
{\mathcal{H}}
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
.

The trivial representation
{\mathbb{C}}

H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)=0\quad\text{for}\ \ \lambda\neq 0,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

H^{i}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{0}\right)\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}&i=0,1\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

H^{i}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}&i=1,2\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

The discrete series representations
D_{n}^{\pm}

(n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1})

H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)=0\quad\text{for}\ \ \lambda\neq\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}

H^{i}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}&i=1,2\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

The principal and complementary series representations
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mu}

(\mu<0)

H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mu})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)=0\quad\text{for}\ \ \lambda\neq\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1+8\mu}}{2\sqrt{2}}

H^{i}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mu})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1+8\mu}}{2\sqrt{2}}}\right)\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}&i=1,2\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

7
Hodge decomposition of a generic part

In this section we will construct an operator
\delta
of degree
-1
on
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)
such that

d\delta+\delta d=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\pi(\Omega)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi\left(-H^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H\right),

where
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathfrak{an}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\xi}}{{\curvearrowright}}V
are representations such that
\xi({\mathfrak{n}})=0
, and
\Omega
is the Casimir element of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
.

Applying to
{\mathcal{H}}=C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})
and
V={\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}
, we obtain a “Hodge decomposition” of
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)
, which shows most part of (ie a large direct summand of)
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)
does not contribute to the cohomology. The rest of
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)
might have a nontrivial cohomology and we will give other “Hodge decompositions” to it in Sections
8
and
9
(except for some cases).

However, to compute
H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})
, together with the results in Section
6.4
, we need only the Hodge decomposition given in this section, which is used to prove an infinite direct sum (a generic part) has a trivial cohomology. So Section
7.3
completes the computation of
H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})
, though the computation results are listed in Section
10
.

7.1
A construction of an operator
\delta
on
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)

In this section we consider the Chevalley–Eilenberg complex
\left(C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V),d\right)
for representations
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\curvearrowright{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathfrak{an}}\curvearrowright V
such that
{\mathfrak{n}}
acts trivially on
V
. We will construct an operator
\delta
on
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)
which decreases the degree by
1
such that
d\delta+\delta d
has a simple form. To make the construction universal, ie independent of the choices of
{\mathcal{H}}
and
V
, we first introduce the algebra

{\mathcal{A}}=C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})\otimes U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))\otimes U({\mathfrak{an}}),

where
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})
is the Clifford algebra of
{\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}
with respect to the symmetric bilinear form coming from the duality of
{\mathfrak{an}}
and
{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}
. (A precise definition is given below.) Then the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
has a natural representation on
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)
and the differential
d
is expressed as an action of an element
\hat{d}_{f}
of
{\mathcal{A}}
, which is independent of
{\mathcal{H}}
and
V
. We will define an element
\hat{\delta}_{f}
of
{\mathcal{A}}
and
\delta
as the action of the element
\hat{\delta}_{f}
on
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)
through the representation. We compute the element
\hat{d}_{f}\hat{\delta}_{f}+\hat{\delta}_{f}\hat{d}_{f}
in
{\mathcal{A}}
and then get a formula for
d\delta+\delta d
on
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{an}}
be the Lie algebra of
AN
and let
{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{an}},{\mathbb{R}})
. Consider the symmetric bilinear form
(\cdot|\cdot)\colon({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})\times({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})\to{\mathbb{R}}
defined by

(X+\varphi|Y+\psi)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\psi(X)+\varphi(Y)\right)

for
X
,
Y\in{\mathfrak{an}}
and
\varphi
,
\psi\in{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}
. Let
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})
denote the Clifford algebra of
\left({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*},(\cdot|\cdot)\right)
. This is defined as the quotient of the tensor algebra over
{\mathbb{R}}
by an ideal:

\displaystyle C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})

\displaystyle=T({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})/\left(v\otimes v-(v|v),v\in{\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=T({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})/\left(v\otimes w+w\otimes v-2(v|w),v,w\in{\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}\right).

Define the maps

\displaystyle\varepsilon(\varphi)\colon\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}

\displaystyle\to\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle\mapsto\varphi\wedge\psi

for
\varphi\in{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}
and

\displaystyle\iota(X)\colon\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}

\displaystyle\to\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle\mapsto\psi(X,\cdots)

for
X\in{\mathfrak{an}}
.

Lemma 26
.

We have
\varepsilon(\varphi)^{2}=0
,
\iota(X)^{2}=0
and
\iota(X)\varepsilon(\varphi)+\varepsilon(\varphi)\iota(X)=\varphi(X)
for all
\varphi\in{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}
and
X\in{\mathfrak{an}}
.

Proof.

The first two equations are obvious and for the third one, take any
\psi\in\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}
and compute as follows:

\displaystyle(\iota(X)\varepsilon(\varphi)+\varepsilon(\varphi)\iota(X))\psi

\displaystyle=\iota(X)(\varphi\wedge\psi)+\varphi\wedge\iota(X)\psi

\displaystyle=\varphi(X)\psi-\varphi\wedge\iota(X)\psi+\varphi\wedge\iota(X)\psi

\displaystyle=\varphi(X)\psi.

∎

Since the linear map

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}

\displaystyle\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\left(\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle X+\varphi

\displaystyle\mapsto\iota(X)+\varepsilon(\varphi)

satisfies

\displaystyle(\iota(X)+\varepsilon(\varphi))(\iota(X)+\varepsilon(\varphi))-(X+\varphi|X+\varphi)

\displaystyle=\iota(X)\varepsilon(\varphi)+\varepsilon(\varphi)\iota(X)-\varphi(X)

\displaystyle=0,

it extends to an algebra homomorphism

\sigma\colon C\left({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}\right)\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\left(\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}\right),

which is called the spin representation of
C\left({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}\right)
. It is well-known that
\sigma
is an isomorphism (at least over
{\mathbb{C}}
but the same proof works over
{\mathbb{R}}
).

Consider the
{\mathbb{R}}
-algebra

{\mathcal{A}}=C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})\otimes U({\mathfrak{sl}}(2,{\mathbb{R}}))\otimes U({\mathfrak{an}}),

where
U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))
(resp.
U({\mathfrak{an}})
) is the universal enveloping algebra of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
(resp.
{\mathfrak{an}}
) over
{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathfrak{an}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\xi}}{{\curvearrowright}}V
be representations such that
\xi({\mathfrak{n}})=0
. Then we get a representation

{\mathcal{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\xi}}{{\curvearrowright}}\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V=C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right).

Recall the elements
H
and
E
of
{\mathfrak{an}}
in Section
3
. Let
\theta_{H}
,
\theta_{E}
be the dual basis of
H
,
E
in
{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}
. Let
d
(resp.
d_{\star}
) be the differential of
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
(resp.
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{R}})
, where
{\mathbb{R}}
is the trivial representation of
{\mathfrak{an}}
). Note that we have
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)=C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{R}})\otimes{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V
. Then the differential
d
is expressed as follows:

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=d_{\star}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\otimes(\pi(H)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(H))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\ +\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\otimes(\pi(E)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(E))

\displaystyle=d_{\star}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\otimes(\pi(H)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(H))+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\otimes\pi(E)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits.

(19)

See for example Maruhashi–Tsutaya
[
12
]
. Let
\hat{d}_{\star}
be the unique element of
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})
such that
\sigma(\hat{d}_{\star})=d_{\star}
. Consider the elements

\hat{d}=\hat{d}_{\star}\otimes 1\otimes 1+\theta_{H}\otimes(H\otimes 1+1\otimes H)+\theta_{E}\otimes(E\otimes 1+1\otimes E)

and

\hat{d}_{f}=\hat{d}_{\star}\otimes 1\otimes 1+\theta_{H}\otimes(H\otimes 1+1\otimes H)+\theta_{E}\otimes E\otimes 1

of
{\mathcal{A}}
. These elements represent the differential
d
:

\displaystyle(\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\xi)(\hat{d})=d,\quad(\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\xi)(\hat{d}_{f})=d.

They are independent of the choice of
{\mathcal{H}}
and
V
. We call
\hat{d}_{f}
the fake differential.

Recall the inner product
B_{\theta}
of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
defined in Section
3
. Its restriction to
{\mathfrak{an}}
gives a linear isomorphism
\kappa\colon{\mathfrak{an}}\to{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}
. Consider a map

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}

\displaystyle\to{\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}

\displaystyle X+\varphi

\displaystyle\mapsto\kappa^{-1}(\varphi)+\kappa(X).

It extends to the unique antihomomorphism

T({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})\to C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})

of algebras. Since we have

\displaystyle(\kappa^{-1}(\varphi)+\kappa(X))(\kappa^{-1}(\varphi)+\kappa(X))

\displaystyle=\kappa^{-1}(\varphi)\kappa(X)+\kappa(X)\kappa^{-1}(\varphi)=\kappa(X)(\kappa^{-1}(\varphi))

\displaystyle=B_{\theta}\left(\kappa^{-1}(\varphi),X\right)=\varphi(X)=(X+\varphi|X+\varphi),

the antihomomorphism factors through
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})
and we get an involutive antiautomorphism

\cdot^{\top}\colon C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})\to C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}).

Since
H
,
E
is an orthonormal basis of
{\mathfrak{an}}
, we have
\kappa(H)=\theta_{H}
,
\kappa(E)=\theta_{E}
, hence
H^{\top}=\theta_{H}
,
E^{\top}=\theta_{E}
in
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})
.

The inner product of
{\mathfrak{an}}
induces a natural inner product of
\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}
. For a linear map
T\colon\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}\to\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}
, let
T^{\top}\colon\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}\to\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}
denote the adjoint of
T
with respect to the inner product.

Lemma 27
.

We have
\sigma(x)^{\top}=\sigma\left(x^{\top}\right)
for all
x\in C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})
.

Proof.

See Maruhashi–Tsutaya
[
12
]
.
∎

We define an element
\hat{\delta}_{f}
of
{\mathcal{A}}
by

\hat{\delta}_{f}=\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}\otimes 1\otimes 1+H\otimes\left(\left(H+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\otimes 1+1\otimes(-H)\right)+E\otimes 2F\otimes 1

and an operator
\delta
on
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)
by

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle=(\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\xi)(\hat{\delta}_{f})

\displaystyle=d_{\star}^{\top}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\iota(H)\otimes\left(\left(\pi(H)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(H)\right)+\iota(E)\otimes 2\pi(F)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits.

The operator
\delta
decreases the degree by
1
.

Remark 28
.

The
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})
-components of
\hat{\delta}_{f}
are obtained from those of
\hat{d}_{f}
by applying
\cdot^{\top}
. The
U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))
-components of
\hat{\delta}_{f}
appear in the expression

\Omega=2FE+\left(H+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)H

of the Casimir element of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
.

If
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
is an
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
-invariant subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}
, then
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\otimes V)
is a subcomplex of
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)
and invariant under
\delta
.

For
X\in{\mathfrak{an}}
, let
L_{X}\colon\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}\to\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}
be the Lie derivative by
X
defined in Section
6.1
. Let
\hat{L}_{X}
be the unique element of
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})
such that
\sigma(\hat{L}_{X})=L_{X}
.

Theorem 29
.

We have

\displaystyle\hat{d}_{f}\hat{\delta}_{f}+\hat{\delta}_{f}\hat{d}_{f}

\displaystyle=\left(\hat{d}_{\star}\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}+\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}\hat{d}_{\star}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\hat{L}_{H}\right)\otimes 1\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad+1\otimes\Omega\otimes 1+1\otimes 1\otimes\left(-H^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H\right)

in
{\mathcal{A}}
, where
\Omega\in U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))
is the Casimir element of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
.

Before starting the proof we list some properties we use in the proof:

•

We have

\hat{d}_{\star}X+X\hat{d}_{\star}=\hat{L}_{X}\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}\kappa(X)+\kappa(X)\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}=\hat{L}_{X}^{\top}

(20)

in
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})
for all
X\in{\mathfrak{an}}
.

•

We have

\hat{L}_{X}=-\theta_{H}[X,H]-\theta_{E}[X,E]

(21)

in
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})
for all
X\in{\mathfrak{an}}
.

•

Since
\hat{L}_{H}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{E}E
, we have

\hat{L}_{H}^{\top}=\hat{L}_{H}.

(22)

See Maruhashi–Tsutaya
[
12
]
for the proofs of the first and second.

Proof of Theorem
29
.

By the definitions

\displaystyle\hat{d}_{f}=\hat{d}_{\star}\otimes 1\otimes 1+\theta_{H}\otimes H\otimes 1+\theta_{H}\otimes 1\otimes H+\theta_{E}\otimes E\otimes 1,

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}_{f}=\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}\otimes 1\otimes 1+H\otimes\left(H+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\otimes 1+H\otimes 1\otimes(-H)+E\otimes 2F\otimes 1

and (
20
), (
22
), we have

\displaystyle\hat{d}_{f}\hat{\delta}_{f}+\hat{\delta}_{f}\hat{d}_{f}

\displaystyle=\left(\hat{d}_{\star}\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}+\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}\hat{d}_{\star}\right)\otimes 1\otimes 1+{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\hat{L}_{H}\otimes\left(H+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\otimes 1}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{L}_{H}\otimes 1\otimes(-H)}

\displaystyle\quad+\hat{L}_{E}\otimes 2F\otimes 1+{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\hat{L}_{H}^{\top}\otimes H\otimes 1}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{L}_{H}^{\top}\otimes 1\otimes H}+\hat{L}_{E}^{\top}\otimes E\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad+1\otimes H\left(H+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\otimes 1+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1\otimes H\otimes(-H)}+\theta_{H}E\otimes 2[H,F]\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad+1\otimes\left({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}H}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\otimes H+1\otimes 1\otimes(-H^{2})+\theta_{E}H\otimes[E,H]\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad+\theta_{E}E\otimes 2[E,F]\otimes 1+1\otimes 2FE\otimes 1

\displaystyle=\left(\hat{d}_{\star}\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}+\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}\hat{d}_{\star}\right)\otimes 1\otimes 1+\hat{L}_{H}\otimes\left(2H+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad+\hat{L}_{E}\otimes 2F\otimes 1+\hat{L}_{E}^{\top}\otimes E\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad+1\otimes H\left(H+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\otimes 1+1\otimes\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\otimes H+1\otimes 1\otimes(-H^{2})+1\otimes 2FE\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad+\theta_{H}E\otimes 2[H,F]\otimes 1+\theta_{E}H\otimes[E,H]\otimes 1+\theta_{E}E\otimes 2[E,F]\otimes 1.

By the formulas

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{H}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{E}E,\quad\hat{L}_{E}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{H}E,

\displaystyle\Omega=2FE+H^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H,

\displaystyle[H,E]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}E,\quad[H,F]=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}F,\quad[E,F]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H,

we get

\displaystyle\hat{d}_{f}\hat{\delta}_{f}+\hat{\delta}_{f}\hat{d}_{f}

\displaystyle=\left(\hat{d}_{\star}\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}+\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}\hat{d}_{\star}\right)\otimes 1\otimes 1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{E}E\otimes\left(2H+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{H}E\otimes 2F\otimes 1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{E}H\otimes E\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad+1\otimes\Omega\otimes 1+1\otimes 1\otimes\left(-H^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\theta_{H}E\otimes\left(-\sqrt{2}F\right)\otimes 1+\theta_{E}H\otimes\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}E\right)\otimes 1+\theta_{E}E\otimes\sqrt{2}H\otimes 1

\displaystyle=\left(\hat{d}_{\star}\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}+\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}\hat{d}_{\star}\right)\otimes 1\otimes 1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{E}E\otimes\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad+1\otimes\Omega\otimes 1+1\otimes 1\otimes\left(-H^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\hat{d}_{\star}\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}+\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}\hat{d}_{\star}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\hat{L}_{H}\right)\otimes 1\otimes 1+1\otimes\Omega\otimes 1+1\otimes 1\otimes\left(-H^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H\right).

∎

Proposition 30
.

We have

\hat{d}_{\star}\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}+\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}\hat{d}_{\star}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\hat{L}_{H}=0

in
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})
.

Proof.

The element
\hat{d}_{\star}
can be expressed as follows:

\hat{d}_{\star}=-\theta_{H}\theta_{E}[H,E]=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{H}\theta_{E}E.

(23)

See Maruhashi–Tsutaya
[
12
]
. Hence we have

\displaystyle 2\left(\hat{d}_{\star}\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}+\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}\hat{d}_{\star}\right)

\displaystyle=\theta_{H}\theta_{E}E\theta_{E}EH+\theta_{E}EH\theta_{H}\theta_{E}E

\displaystyle=\theta_{H}\theta_{E}EH-\theta_{E}E\theta_{H}H\theta_{E}E+\theta_{E}E\theta_{E}E

\displaystyle=\theta_{H}\theta_{E}EH+\theta_{E}\theta_{H}EH\theta_{E}E+\theta_{E}E

\displaystyle=\theta_{H}\theta_{E}EH-\theta_{E}\theta_{H}E\theta_{E}HE+\theta_{E}E

\displaystyle=\theta_{H}\theta_{E}EH-\theta_{E}\theta_{H}HE+\theta_{E}E

\displaystyle=\theta_{E}E

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{2}\hat{L}_{H}.

∎

By Theorem
29
and Proposition
30
we get the following theorem.

Theorem 31
.

We have

\hat{d}_{f}\hat{\delta}_{f}+\hat{\delta}_{f}\hat{d}_{f}=1\otimes\Omega\otimes 1+1\otimes 1\otimes\left(-H^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H\right)

in
{\mathcal{A}}
, hence

d\delta+\delta d=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\pi(\Omega)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\left(-\xi(H)^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\xi(H)\right)

on
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)
.

Proposition 32
.

We have
\hat{\delta}_{f}^{2}=0
as an element of
{\mathcal{A}}
, hence
\delta^{2}=0
on
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)
.

In the proof we use the following properties:

•

We have
\hat{d}_{\star}^{2}=0
in
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})
.

•

There is an injective algebra homomorphism

j\colon\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}\hookrightarrow C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})

which is the identity on
{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}
.

•

We have

\hat{d}_{\star}\varphi+\varphi\hat{d}_{\star}=j(d_{\star}\varphi)

(24)

in
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})
for all
\varphi\in{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}
. See Maruhashi–Tsutaya
[
12
]
.

•

We have

d_{\star}\theta_{H}=0,\quad d_{\star}\theta_{E}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{H}\wedge\theta_{E}.

(25)

Proof of Proposition
32
.

By (
24
) and (
25
) we get

\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}H+H\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}=0,\quad\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}E+E\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}EH.

Using the definition

\hat{\delta}_{f}=\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}\otimes 1\otimes 1+H\otimes\left(H+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\otimes 1+H\otimes 1\otimes(-H)+E\otimes 2F\otimes 1,

we have

\hat{\delta}_{f}^{2}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}EH\otimes 2F\otimes 1+HE\otimes 2[H,F]\otimes 1=0.

∎

Remark 33
.

We can also show
\hat{d}^{2}=0
and
\hat{d}_{f}^{2}=0
in
{\mathcal{A}}
.

7.2
Hodge decomposition of
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})
for generic
{\mathcal{H}}

In this section we give a “Hodge decomposition” of
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})
for “generic”
{\mathcal{H}}
by using the operator
\delta
constructed in Section
7.1
.

Recall that for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
the representation
{\mathfrak{an}}\curvearrowright{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}
is defined by

\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}={\mathbb{C}},\quad H1=\lambda 1,\quad E1=0.

Let
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{H}}
be a representation and consider the Chevalley–Eilenberg complex
\left(C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}),d\right)
. Then by Theorem
31
we have an operator
\delta
on
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})
of degree
-1
such that

\displaystyle d\delta+\delta d

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\pi(\Omega)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\left(-\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda\right)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\left(\pi(\Omega)-\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda\right)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits.

Assume that the operator

\pi(\Omega)-\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda\colon{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}

is bijective. Put

Q=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\left(\pi(\Omega)-\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda\right)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
, so that
d\delta+\delta d=Q
. Then we have
dQ=Qd
and
\delta Q=Q\delta
since
\Omega
lies in the center of
U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))
. Hence we get
dQ^{-1}=Q^{-1}d
and
\delta Q^{-1}=Q^{-1}\delta
. Put

\delta^{\prime}=Q^{-1}\delta\colon C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})\to C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}).

So we have

\displaystyle d\delta^{\prime}+\delta^{\prime}d=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,\quad(\delta^{\prime})^{2}=0,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta^{\prime}=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta,\quad\ker\delta^{\prime}=\ker\delta,

which imply

\displaystyle C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta^{\prime},

\displaystyle\ker d=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d,\quad\ker\delta^{\prime}=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta^{\prime},

\displaystyle H^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})=0.

When the operator
\pi(\Omega)-\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda
is not necessarily bijective on
{\mathcal{H}}
, let

{\mathcal{H}}^{0}=\ker\left(\pi(\Omega)-\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda\right).

Then
{\mathcal{H}}^{0}
is an
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
-invariant subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}
. Assume that there exists an
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
-invariant subspace
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{H}}
such that
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}^{0}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
and the operator

\pi(\Omega)-\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda\colon{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}

is bijective. Then we have

C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})=C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}^{0}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})\oplus C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})

and
H^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})=0
. Hence

\displaystyle H^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=H^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}^{0}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})\oplus H^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=H^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}^{0}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}).

Note that we have
d\delta+\delta d=0
on
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}^{0}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})
.

7.3
Computation of
H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})

To compute the cohomology
H^{*}\left({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)
for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
, we use the isomorphism

H^{*}\left({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)\simeq H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)

in Proposition
3
. Note that
C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})
is not just a representation of
{\mathfrak{an}}
but also that of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. Let
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})
denote the representation. We apply things in Section
7.2
. First we put

C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})^{0}=\left\{f\in C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\ \middle|\ \pi(\Omega)f=\left(\lambda^{2}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda\right)f\right\}

following a notation in Section
7.2
.

Recall that we have a unitary representation
\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\curvearrowright L^{2}(P)
. By (
9
) in Section
5.3
we have a decomposition

L^{2}(P)=\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Upsilon}W_{\mu}=W_{\lambda^{2}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda}\oplus\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Upsilon_{\lambda}}W_{\mu},

where

\Upsilon=\left\{-\frac{\nu}{2}\ \middle|\ \nu\in\sigma({\overline{\Delta_{\Sigma}}})\right\}\cup\left\{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\ \middle|\ n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}\right\},

\Upsilon_{\lambda}=\Upsilon\setminus\left\{\lambda^{2}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda\right\}

and
W_{\mu}\subset L^{2}(P)
is the sum of the irreducible unitary subrepresentations of
L^{2}(P)
with Casimir parameter
\mu
. By taking the space of
C^{\infty}
vectors of both sides, we get a decomposition

C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})=C^{\infty}\left(W_{\lambda^{2}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda}\right)\oplus C^{\infty}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Upsilon_{\lambda}}W_{\mu}\right)

into
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
-invariant subspaces. We use this as the decomposition
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}^{0}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
in Section
7.2
. First note that we have

C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})^{0}=C^{\infty}\left(W_{\lambda^{2}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda}\right).

So we need to prove the following to apply results in Section
7.2
.

Lemma 34
.

The operator

\pi(\Omega)-\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda\colon C^{\infty}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Upsilon_{\lambda}}W_{\mu}\right)\to C^{\infty}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Upsilon_{\lambda}}W_{\mu}\right)

is bijective.

To prove this lemma we use the following description of the space of
C^{\infty}
vectors.

Lemma 35
.

For
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
let
\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\curvearrowright{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
be a unitary representation and
\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{H}}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\infty}{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
be the direct sum. Then we have

C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}f_{i}\ \middle|\ f_{i}\in C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}_{i}),\ \sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert Xf_{i}\rVert^{2}<\infty\ \ \text{for all $X\in U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))$}\right\}.

Moreover, if
f\in C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
and
f=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}f_{i}
in
{\mathcal{H}}
with
f_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
, we have
Xf=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}Xf_{i}
in
{\mathcal{H}}
for all
X\in U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))
.

Proof.

Let
p_{i}\colon{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
be the projection. Let
f\in C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
and
f=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}f_{i}
in
{\mathcal{H}}
with
f_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
. Since
p_{i}
is bounded and
\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
-equivariant, the component
f_{i}=p_{i}(f)
is an element of
C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}_{i})
. Take
X\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. We have
Xf=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}g_{i}
for some
g_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
. Since
\pi
is continuous, we have
\pi(e^{tX})f=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\pi(e^{tX})f_{i}
for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Hence

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}g_{i}=Xf=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\pi(e^{tX})f-f}{t}=\lim_{t\to 0}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\pi(e^{tX})f_{i}-f_{i}}{t}.

Applying
p_{i}
to this equation, we get

g_{i}=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\pi(e^{tX})f_{i}-f_{i}}{t}=Xf_{i}.

Thus
Xf=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}Xf_{i}
. We see that this holds for all
X\in U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))
, by repeating the argument. In particular, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert Xf_{i}\rVert^{2}<\infty
.

Conversely let
f=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}f_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
be such that
f_{i}\in C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}_{i})
and
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lVert Xf_{i}\rVert^{2}<\infty
for all
X\in U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))
. Take
X\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. By assumption we have an element
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}Xf_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
. We prove that
Xf
exists and
Xf=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}Xf_{i}
. First we have

\frac{\pi(e^{tX})f-f}{t}-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}Xf_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\pi(e^{tX})f_{i}-f_{i}}{t}-Xf_{i}\right).

Put
c(t)=\pi(e^{tX})f_{i}
. Then
c\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{i}
is a
C^{\infty}
map. The fundamental theorem of calculus holds for functions with values in a Fréchet space, hence a Hilbert space in particular. See for example 2.2.2 of Hamilton
[
9
]
. So we get the following form of Taylor’s theorem by integration by parts:

\displaystyle c(t)-c(0)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}c^{\prime}(s)ds=tc^{\prime}(t)-\int_{0}^{t}sc^{\prime\prime}(s)ds

\displaystyle=tc^{\prime}(0)+t\left(c^{\prime}(t)-c^{\prime}(0)\right)-\int_{0}^{t}sc^{\prime\prime}(s)ds

\displaystyle=tc^{\prime}(0)+t\int_{0}^{t}c^{\prime\prime}(s)ds-\int_{0}^{t}sc^{\prime\prime}(s)ds

\displaystyle=tc^{\prime}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)c^{\prime\prime}(s)ds.

Thus we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{\pi(e^{tX})f_{i}-f_{i}}{t}-Xf_{i}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\int_{0}^{t}\left(1-\frac{s}{t}\right)\pi(e^{sX})X^{2}f_{i}ds\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\left(1-\frac{s}{t}\right)\left\lVert X^{2}f_{i}\right\rVert ds\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert X^{2}f_{i}\right\rVert\left\lvert\left[s-\frac{s^{2}}{2t}\right]_{0}^{t}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert X^{2}f_{i}\right\rVert\frac{\lvert t\rvert}{2}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{\pi(e^{tX})f-f}{t}-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}Xf_{i}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\lVert\frac{\pi(e^{tX})f_{i}-f_{i}}{t}-Xf_{i}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t^{2}}{4}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left\lVert X^{2}f_{i}\right\rVert^{2}\to 0\quad(t\to 0).

Hence we proved
Xf=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}Xf_{i}
. By repeating the argument, we see that this holds for all
X\in U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))
. From this we can show that the map

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})

\displaystyle\to{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto\pi(g)f

is a
C^{\infty}
map, hence
f\in C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
34
.

We have

C^{\infty}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Upsilon_{\lambda}}W_{\mu}\right)=\left\{\sum_{\mu}f_{\mu}\ \middle|\ \begin{gathered}f_{\mu}\in C^{\infty}(W_{\mu}),\\
\sum_{\mu}\lVert Xf_{\mu}\rVert^{2}<\infty\ \text{for all $X\in U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))$}\end{gathered}\right\}.

by Lemma
35
and

\displaystyle\pi(\Omega)-\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda\colon C^{\infty}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Upsilon_{\lambda}}W_{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\to C^{\infty}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Upsilon_{\lambda}}W_{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu}f_{\mu}

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{\mu}\left(\mu-\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda\right)f_{\mu}.

Since the set
\Upsilon
has no accumulation points in
{\mathbb{R}}
, the quantity
\left(\mu-\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda\right)^{-1}
is bounded for
\mu\in\Upsilon_{\lambda}
. By this and the description of
C^{\infty}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Upsilon_{\lambda}}W_{\mu}\right)
, we can define an operator

\displaystyle C^{\infty}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Upsilon_{\lambda}}W_{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\to C^{\infty}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Upsilon_{\lambda}}W_{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu}f_{\mu}

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{\mu}\left(\mu-\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda\right)^{-1}f_{\mu},

which is the inverse of
\pi(\Omega)-\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda
.
∎

Therefore, by Section
7.2
, we obtain the following.

Theorem 36
.

For any
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
, we have a decomposition

C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})=C_{\lambda}\oplus C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Upsilon_{\lambda}}W_{\mu}\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)

of the cochain complex, where we put

C_{\lambda}=C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}\left(W_{\lambda^{2}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda}\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)

and a “Hodge decomposition”

C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Upsilon_{\lambda}}W_{\mu}\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta.

Hence

H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Upsilon_{\lambda}}W_{\mu}\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)=0

and

H^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})=H^{*}(C_{\lambda})=H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}\left(W_{\lambda^{2}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda}\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right).

Let us see when
C_{\lambda}=0
. Put
\Lambda=\left\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}\ \middle|\ \lambda^{2}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda\in\Upsilon\right\}
. Then

C_{\lambda}=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad W_{\lambda^{2}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda}=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\lambda^{2}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda\not\in\Upsilon\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\lambda\not\in\Lambda.

Recall

\Upsilon=\left\{-\frac{\nu}{2}\ \middle|\ \nu\in\sigma({\overline{\Delta_{\Sigma}}})\right\}\cup\left\{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\ \middle|\ n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}\right\}.

Since

\lambda^{2}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda=-\frac{\nu}{2}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\lambda=\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1-4\nu}}{2\sqrt{2}},

\lambda^{2}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda=\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\lambda=\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}},

we have

\Lambda=\left\{\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1-4\nu}}{2\sqrt{2}}\ \middle|\ \nu\in\sigma({\overline{\Delta_{\Sigma}}})\right\}\cup\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{\mathbb{Z}}.

Corollary 37
.

We have

H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})\simeq H^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})=0

for any

\lambda\not\in\Lambda=\left\{\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1-4\nu}}{2\sqrt{2}}\ \middle|\ \nu\in\sigma({\overline{\Delta_{\Sigma}}})\right\}\cup\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{\mathbb{Z}}.

Let us give a decomposition of
C_{\lambda}=C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}\left(W_{\lambda^{2}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda}\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)
for
\lambda\in\Lambda
. By Section
5.3.4
, we have

\displaystyle C_{0}

\displaystyle=C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(W_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\simeq C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})\oplus g_{\Sigma}C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{+}))\oplus g_{\Sigma}C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{-}))

and

\displaystyle C_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}

\displaystyle=C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(W_{0})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)\oplus g_{\Sigma}C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{+})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)\oplus g_{\Sigma}C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{-})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right),

where
g_{\Sigma}
is the genus of the surface
\Sigma=\Gamma\backslash\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})/\mathop{\mathrm{PSO}}\nolimits(2)
. For
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 2}
, we have

\displaystyle C_{\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}}

\displaystyle=C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}\left(W_{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq(2n-1)(g_{\Sigma}-1)\left(C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{+})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)\oplus C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{-})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)\right)

and

\displaystyle C_{\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}}

\displaystyle=C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}\left(W_{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq(2n-1)(g_{\Sigma}-1)\left(C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{+})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)\oplus C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{-})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)\right).

(26)

For
\nu\in\sigma({\overline{\Delta_{\Sigma}}})\setminus\{0\}
, we have

\displaystyle C_{\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1-4\nu}}{2\sqrt{2}}}

\displaystyle=C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}\left(W_{-\frac{\nu}{2}}\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1-4\nu}}{2\sqrt{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq m_{\nu}C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}\left({\mathcal{H}}_{-\frac{\nu}{2}}\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1-4\nu}}{2\sqrt{2}}}\right),

where
m_{\nu}
is the multiplicity of the eigenvalue
\nu
of
{\overline{\Delta_{\Sigma}}}
.

By these decompositions of
C_{\lambda}
for
\lambda\in\Lambda
and the cohomology computation results in Section
6.4
, we can now compute
H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
. The results are listed in Section
10
. However, we can not just compute the cohomology but also give “Hodge decompositions” of the cochain complexes for most
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
. So before going to Section
10
, we will give “Hodge decompositions” that are not covered by Section
7.2
, in the next two sections.

Among the complexes
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})
, those that are not covered by the Hodge decomposition in Section
7.2
are

C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})\quad\text{and}\quad C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right).

Constructions of Hodge decompositions for these are easy and given in Section
8
.

Among
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)
, those that are not covered by the Hodge decompositions in Section
7.2
are

C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right).

Hodge decompositions for these complexes will be constructed in Section
9
.

Among
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}\left({\mathcal{H}}_{-\frac{\nu}{2}}\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)
for
\nu<0
, those that are not covered by the Hodge decompositions in Section
7.2
are

C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}\left({\mathcal{H}}_{-\frac{\nu}{2}}\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1-4\nu}}{2\sqrt{2}}}\right).

But we did not find a “Hodge decomposition” for these complexes.

8
Hodge decompositions of
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})
and
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)

In this section we construct “Hodge decompositions” of
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})
and
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)
. Since they are finite dimensional, the constructions are easy. We omit the detailed calculations in this section.

Note that a “Hodge decomposition” of
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})
for
\lambda\neq 0,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
is already given in Section
7.2
.

8.1
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})

Consider the following basis of
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})
:

\displaystyle 1,\ \ \ \theta_{H},\ \ \ \theta_{E},\ \ \ \theta_{H}\wedge\theta_{E}.

We have

\displaystyle d1=0,\quad d\theta_{H}=0,\quad d\theta_{E}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{H}\wedge\theta_{E},\quad d(\theta_{H}\wedge\theta_{E})=0

for the differential
d
of
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})
. Hence

d_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad d_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\end{pmatrix},

where
d_{i}
is the differential on
C^{i}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})
. Let
\delta_{i}\colon C^{i}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})\to C^{i-1}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})
and
p_{i}\colon C^{i}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})\to C^{i}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})
be linear maps. Then
\delta_{i}
and
p_{i}
satisfy

\displaystyle d\delta+\delta d=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-p,\quad p^{2}=p,\quad dp=0,\quad\delta p=0,\quad pd=0,\quad p\delta=0,\quad\delta^{2}=0

if and only if

\displaystyle\delta_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\delta_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}a\\
-\sqrt{2}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle p_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}1\end{pmatrix},\quad p_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}a\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad p_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0\end{pmatrix}

for some
a\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Using these
\delta
and
p
, we get a “Hodge decomposition” of
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})
:

\displaystyle C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta

\displaystyle\ker d

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d

\displaystyle\ker\delta

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta

\displaystyle\ker d\cap\ker\delta

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta

\displaystyle=\ker p.

Hence

H^{i}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p_{i}=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}1&i=0\\
{\mathbb{C}}\theta_{H}&i=1\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

As in Section
7.1
it is possible to give a degree free expression of
\delta
by using the Clifford algebra. Let
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})
be the Clifford algebra of
{\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}
and
\sigma
be the representation
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\curvearrowright C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})
obtained from the spin representation
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})\curvearrowright C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{R}})
. Define elements
\hat{\delta}
and
\hat{p}
of
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
by

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}

\displaystyle=\left(\sqrt{2}\theta_{E}-a\theta_{H}\right)HE

\displaystyle\hat{p}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\sqrt{2}\theta_{E}-a\theta_{H}\right)E.

Then we have
\sigma(\hat{\delta})=\delta
and
\sigma(\hat{p})=p
.

8.2
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)

Consider the following basis of
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)
:

\displaystyle 1,\ \ \ \theta_{H},\ \ \ \theta_{E},\ \ \ \theta_{H}\wedge\theta_{E}.

We have

\displaystyle d1=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{H},\quad d\theta_{H}=0,\quad d\theta_{E}=0,\quad d(\theta_{H}\wedge\theta_{E})=0

for the differential
d
of
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)
. Hence

d_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad d_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\end{pmatrix},

where
d_{i}
is the differential on
C^{i}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)
. Let
\delta_{i}\colon C^{i}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)\to C^{i-1}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)
and
p_{i}\colon C^{i}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)\to C^{i}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)
be linear maps. Then
\delta_{i}
and
p_{i}
satisfy

\displaystyle d\delta+\delta d=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-p,\quad p^{2}=p,\quad dp=0,\quad\delta p=0,\quad pd=0,\quad p\delta=0,\quad\delta^{2}=0

if and only if

\displaystyle\delta_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{2}&a\end{pmatrix},\quad\delta_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle p_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}0\end{pmatrix},\quad p_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}a\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad p_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}1\end{pmatrix}

for some
a\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Using these
\delta
and
p
, we get a “Hodge decomposition” of
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)
:

\displaystyle C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta

\displaystyle\ker d

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d

\displaystyle\ker\delta

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta

\displaystyle\ker d\cap\ker\delta

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta

\displaystyle=\ker p.

Hence

H^{i}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p_{i}=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}a\theta_{H}+\theta_{E}\right)&i=1\\
{\mathbb{C}}(\theta_{H}\wedge\theta_{E})&i=2\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Let
\sigma
be the representation
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\curvearrowright C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)
obtained from the spin representation
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})\curvearrowright C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{R}})
. Define elements
\hat{\delta}
and
\hat{p}
of
C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
by

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}

\displaystyle=HE\left(\sqrt{2}\theta_{E}-a\theta_{H}\right)

\displaystyle\hat{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\sqrt{2}\theta_{E}-a\theta_{H}\right)E.

Then
\sigma(\hat{\delta})=\delta
and
\sigma(\hat{p})=p
.

9
Hodge decomposition of
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})

In this section we consider the cochain complex
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})
and give a “Hodge decomposition” of it. There are three sections here but Section
9.1
is just a restatement of the Hodge decomposition given in Section
7.2
and not necessary because Sections
9.2
and
9.3
cover all the cases. We included Section
9.1
just to compare it with Section
9.2
and Section
9.3
.

9.1
\lambda\neq\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}

First we consider the case when
\lambda\neq\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}
. We just apply things in Section
7.1
and Section
7.2
. Let
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})
denote the derivative of the discrete series representation. Consider the operator

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle=d_{\star}^{\top}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\iota(H)\otimes\left(\pi(H)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}-\lambda\right)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\iota(E)\otimes 2\pi(F)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

of degree
-1
on
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})
, constructed in Section
7.1
. Since

\displaystyle\pi(\Omega)-\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda

\displaystyle=\frac{n(n-1)}{2}-\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda

\displaystyle=-\left(\lambda-\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\left(\lambda-\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\neq 0,

the operator

\pi(\Omega)-\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lambda\colon C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\to C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})

is bijective. So by Section
7.2
we have

C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta

and

H^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})=0.

9.2
\lambda\neq\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}

Here we will construct a “Hodge decomposition” of
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)
when
\lambda\neq\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}
. So a newly covered case is
\lambda=\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}
. Even for already covered cases, the construction here is different from the one in Sections
7.1
and
7.2
.

First we consider a bit more general cochain complex
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)
just to make the argument clearer and apply the results to
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)
later.

9.2.1
A general consideration

Let
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{H}}
be a representation on a complex vector space
{\mathcal{H}}
. Assume that
\pi(E)\colon{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}
is injective and there exists a linear map
\varphi\colon{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\ker\varphi=\pi(E){\mathcal{H}}
. Let
{\mathfrak{an}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\xi}}{{\curvearrowright}}V
be a real (resp. complex) representation such that
\xi({\mathfrak{n}})=0
. Consider the tensor product
{\mathfrak{an}}\curvearrowright{\mathcal{H}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}V
(resp.
{\mathcal{H}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V
) of the representations. We will construct a “Hodge decomposition” of the Chevalley–Eilenberg complex
\left(C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V),d\right)
assuming one more condition which amounts to
\lambda\neq\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathbb{C}})
be the complex dual and
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{H}}^{*}
be the representation defined by
(\pi(X)\psi)f=-\psi(\pi(X)f)
for
X\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
,
\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}^{*}
and
f\in{\mathcal{H}}
. Consider the zeroth cohomology

\displaystyle H^{0}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}^{*})

\displaystyle=\left\{\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}^{*}\ \middle|\ \pi(E)\psi=0\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\psi\in{\mathcal{H}}^{*}\ \middle|\ \psi(\pi(E){\mathcal{H}})=0\right\}.

Since the subspace
\pi(E){\mathcal{H}}
is of codimension
1
or
0
in
{\mathcal{H}}
by the assumption
\ker\varphi=\pi(E){\mathcal{H}}
, we have
H^{0}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}^{*})={\mathbb{C}}\varphi
. Since
{\mathfrak{n}}={\mathbb{R}}E
is an ideal of
{\mathfrak{an}}
, the representation
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{H}}^{*}
restricts to a representation
{\mathfrak{an}}\curvearrowright H^{0}({\mathfrak{n}};{\mathcal{H}}^{*})
. It follows that
\pi(H)\varphi=\nu\varphi
for some
\nu\in{\mathbb{C}}
.

For any
f\in{\mathcal{H}}
, we have

\varphi\left((\pi(H)+\nu)f\right)=-\left(\pi(H)\varphi\right)(f)+\nu\varphi(f)=0.

So we can consider an element
\pi(E)^{-1}(\pi(H)+\nu)f\in{\mathcal{H}}
by the assumptions. Define an operator
S\colon{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}
by

S=\pi(E)^{-1}(\pi(H)+\nu).

Consider the operator

\delta=\iota(H)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\iota(E)\otimes S\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

(27)

on
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)=\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V
of degree
-1
. We have
\delta^{2}=0
. To compute
d\delta+\delta d
we need some formulas. First we obviously have
\pi(E)S=\pi(H)+\nu
.

Lemma 38
.

We have
S\pi(E)=\pi(H)+\nu+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle S\pi(E)

\displaystyle=\pi(E)^{-1}(\pi(H)+\nu)\pi(E)=\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\pi(H)\pi(E)+\nu\pi(E)\right)

\displaystyle=\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\pi\left([H,E]\right)+\pi(E)\pi(H)+\nu\pi(E)\right)

\displaystyle=\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\pi\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}E\right)+\pi(E)\pi(H)+\nu\pi(E)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}+\pi(H)+\nu.

∎

Thus

[\pi(E),S]=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}.

(28)

Since
{\mathfrak{n}}={\mathbb{R}}E
is an ideal of
{\mathfrak{an}}
, the representation
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{H}}
restricts to
{\mathfrak{an}}\curvearrowright\pi(E){\mathcal{H}}
. Hence the operator
\pi(E)^{-1}\pi(H)
is defined on
\pi(E){\mathcal{H}}
.

Lemma 39
.

We have
\pi(E)^{-1}\pi(H)-\pi(H)\pi(E)^{-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\pi(E)^{-1}
on
\pi(E){\mathcal{H}}
.

Proof.

For
f\in{\mathcal{H}}
, put
f^{\prime}=\pi(E)f
. We have

\pi(H)\pi(E)f=\pi(E)\pi(H)f+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\pi(E)f,

hence

\pi(E)^{-1}\pi(H)\pi(E)f=\pi(H)f+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}f,

that is,

\pi(E)^{-1}\pi(H)f^{\prime}=\pi(H)\pi(E)^{-1}f^{\prime}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\pi(E)^{-1}f^{\prime}.

∎

Lemma 40
.

We have
[\pi(H),S]=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}S
.

Proof.

By Lemma
39
, we have

\displaystyle\pi(H)S

\displaystyle=\pi(H)\pi(E)^{-1}(\pi(H)+\nu)

\displaystyle=\left(\pi(E)^{-1}\pi(H)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\pi(E)^{-1}\right)(\pi(H)+\nu)

\displaystyle=\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\pi(H)^{2}+\left(\nu-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\pi(H)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\nu\right).

Since
S\pi(H)=\pi(E)^{-1}(\pi(H)+\nu)\pi(H)
, we get

[\pi(H),S]=\pi(E)^{-1}\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\pi(H)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\nu\right)=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}S.

∎

Recall that the differential
d
of
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)
can be expressed as

d=d_{\star}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\otimes\pi(H)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(H)+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\otimes\pi(E)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

by the assumption
\xi(E)=0
, where
d_{\star}
is the differential of
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{R}})
. See (
7.1
) in Section
7.1
.

Theorem 41
.

We have

d\delta+\delta d=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(H)-\left(\nu+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits.

Proof.

Recalling the definition

\delta=\iota(H)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\iota(E)\otimes S\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

and using Lemmas
26
,
38
and
40
, Formulas (
20
) and (
21
) in Section
7.1
and Equation (
28
), we get

\displaystyle d\delta+\delta d

\displaystyle=L_{H}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\pi(H)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(H)-L_{E}\otimes S\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle\quad-\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes[\pi(H),S]\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\pi(E)S\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)\otimes S\pi(E)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\right)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\pi(H)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(H)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes S\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}S\right)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle\quad-\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes[\pi(E),S]\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\left(\pi(H)+\nu+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(H)-\left(\nu+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits.

∎

If
\xi(H)
is a scalar multiplication, we have

d\delta+\delta d=\left(\xi(H)-\nu-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits.

Consequently we get the following theorem.

Theorem 42
.

Let
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{H}}
be a representation on a complex vector space
{\mathcal{H}}
such that
\pi(E)\colon{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}
is injective and there exists a linear map
\varphi\colon{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
with
\ker\varphi=\pi(E){\mathcal{H}}
. Take
\nu\in{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying
\pi(H)\varphi=\nu\varphi
. Let
{\mathfrak{an}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\xi}}{{\curvearrowright}}V
be a real (resp. complex) representation such that
\xi(E)=0
and
\xi(H)
acts as a scalar multiplication satisfying
\xi(H)\neq\nu+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
. Define an operator
\delta
on
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)
by (
27
). Then we have

C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta

by

x=\left(\xi(H)-\nu-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{-1}d\delta x+\left(\xi(H)-\nu-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{-1}\delta dx

and

\ker d=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d,\quad\ker\delta=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta.

Hence
H^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)=0
.

9.2.2
Application to
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)

Let
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})
be the derivative of the discrete series representation. The operator
\pi(E)
is injective by (
6.3
) in Section
6.3
. By Proposition
22
, there exists a linear map
\varphi\colon C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\to{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\ker\varphi=\pi(E)C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})
and
\pi(H)\varphi=-\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}\varphi
. So
\nu=-\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}
and we define an operator

S=\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\pi(H)-\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}\right)

on
C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})
and an operator

\delta=\iota(H)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\iota(E)\otimes S\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

on
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)
. By Theorem
41
we have

d\delta+\delta d=\left(\lambda-\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits.

Theorem 43
.

If
\lambda\neq\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}
, we have

C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta

by

x=\left(\lambda-\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{-1}d\delta x+\left(\lambda-\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{-1}\delta dx

and

\ker d=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d,\quad\ker\delta=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta.

In particular,
H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)=0
.

9.3
\lambda=\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}

Finally we will construct a “Hodge decomposition” of
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)
. In this case the cohomology is nontrivial as we saw in Section
6.4
.

9.3.1
A general consideration

Let
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{H}}
be a representation on a complex vector space
{\mathcal{H}}
. Assume that
\pi(E)\colon{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}
is injective and there exist a linear map
\varphi\colon{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
and an element
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
such that
\ker\varphi=\pi(E){\mathcal{H}}
and
\varphi(h)=1
. Let
{\mathfrak{an}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\xi}}{{\curvearrowright}}V
be a real or complex representation such that
\xi({\mathfrak{n}})=0
. In this section we will construct a “Hodge decomposition” of
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)
assuming one more condition which amounts to
\lambda=\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}
.

Since
f-\varphi(f)h\in\ker\varphi=\pi(E){\mathcal{H}}
for all
f\in{\mathcal{H}}
, we can define an operator
T\colon{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}
by

Tf=\pi(E)^{-1}(f-\varphi(f)h).

Consider an operator

\delta=\iota(E)\otimes T\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

on
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)=\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V
of degree
-1
. Obviously
\delta^{2}=0
. We will prove this
\delta
gives a “Hodge decomposition” of
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)
under one more condition. First we have
\pi(E)T=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\varphi h
and
T\pi(E)=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
, hence
[\pi(E),T]=-\varphi h
. As we have shown in Section
9.2.1
, the assumption
\ker\varphi=\pi(E){\mathcal{H}}
implies that there exists
\nu\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\pi(H)\varphi=\nu\varphi
. Since

\varphi\left(\pi(H)h+\nu h\right)=-(\pi(H)\varphi)(h)+\nu=0,

the image of the operator
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\varphi h
is contained in
\pi(E){\mathcal{H}}
. So we can define an element
h^{\prime}=\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\pi(H)h+\nu h\right)\in{\mathcal{H}}
.

Lemma 44
.

We have

[\pi(H),T]=-\varphi h^{\prime}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}T.

Proof.

By Lemma
39
we have

\displaystyle\pi(H)T

\displaystyle=\pi(H)\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\varphi h\right)

\displaystyle=\pi(E)^{-1}\pi(H)\left(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\varphi h\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\varphi h\right)

\displaystyle=\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\pi(H)-\varphi\pi(H)h\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}T.

On the other hand

\displaystyle T\pi(H)

\displaystyle=\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\varphi h\right)\pi(H)=\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\pi(H)+\left(\pi(H)\varphi\right)h\right)

\displaystyle=\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\pi(H)+\varphi\nu h\right),

hence

[\pi(H),T]=\pi(E)^{-1}\left(-\varphi\pi(H)h-\varphi\nu h\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}T=-\varphi h^{\prime}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}T.

∎

Lemma 45
.

We have

d\delta+\delta d=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-p,

where
p\colon C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\to C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)
is defined by

p=\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits.

Proof.

Recalling

d=d_{\star}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\otimes\pi(H)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(H)+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\otimes\pi(E)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

and using Lemmas
26
and
44
and Formulas (
20
) and (
21
) in Section
7.1
, we have

\displaystyle d\delta+\delta d
