\displaystyle=L_{E}\otimes T\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes[\pi(H),T]\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\pi(E)T\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle\quad+(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E))\otimes T\pi(E)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes T\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes\left(-\varphi h^{\prime}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}T\right)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes(-\varphi h)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-p.

∎

Lemma 46
.

We have
p^{2}=p
. Hence

\displaystyle C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p\oplus\ker p

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=px+(x-px).

Proof.

Using Lemma
26
and
\varphi(h)=1
, we have

\displaystyle p^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi(\varphi h)h^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi(\varphi h)h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle=p.

∎

Obviously
\ker d\cap\ker\delta\subset\ker(d\delta+\delta d)
. We consider whether the opposite inclusion holds.

Lemma 47
.

\delta p=0
.

Proof.

Using Lemma
26
and
\varphi(h)=1
, we have

\displaystyle\delta p

\displaystyle=\left(\iota(E)\otimes T\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)\left(\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)

\displaystyle=\iota(E)\otimes\left(\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\varphi h\right)\circ\varphi h\right)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle=\iota(E)\otimes\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\varphi h-\varphi(\varphi h)h\right)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle=0.

∎

Lemma 48
.

If
\xi(H)=\nu+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
, then
dp=0
.

Proof.

By (
23
) in Section
7.1
we have

d_{\star}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E).

Recalling
h^{\prime}=\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\pi(H)h+\nu h\right)
, we have

\displaystyle dp

\displaystyle=\left(d_{\star}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\otimes\pi(H)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(H)+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\otimes\pi(E)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)

\displaystyle\quad\ \left(\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi\pi(E)h^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi\pi(H)h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\xi(H)

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\left(\varphi\pi(H)h+\nu\varphi h\right)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi\pi(H)h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\xi(H)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\left(-\left(\nu+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varphi h\otimes\xi(H)\right)

\displaystyle=0.

∎

Therefore, by Lemmas
45
,
47
and
48
, if
\xi(H)=\nu+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
, we have

\ker d\cap\ker\delta=\ker(d\delta+\delta d).

Without assuming
\xi(H)=\nu+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
we have:

•

\ker p\subset\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d+\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta
by Lemma
45

•

p\delta=0
immediately from the definitions

•

\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d+\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta
.

For the third one, if
x\in\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d\cap\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta
, then
x\in\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p
by Lemma
45
and
x\in\ker p
by
p\delta=0
. Since
\ker p\cap\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p=0
, we have
x=0
.

The following lemma gives a condition under which we have
\ker p=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta
.

Lemma 49
.

If
\xi(H)=\nu+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
, then
pd=0
.

Proof.

By the formula

d_{\star}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)

and
\varphi\left(\pi(E){\mathcal{H}}\right)=0
, we have

\displaystyle pd

\displaystyle=\left(\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)

\displaystyle\quad\ \left(d_{\star}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\otimes\pi(H)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(H)+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\otimes\pi(E)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\otimes\varphi(\pi(H))h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\otimes\varphi h\otimes\xi(H)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle\quad-\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes\left(-\left(\pi(H)\varphi\right)h\right)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\xi(H)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes\nu\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\xi(H)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes\left(\left(\nu+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\varphi h\otimes\xi(H)\right)

\displaystyle=0.

∎

Thus if
\xi(H)=\nu+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
, we have
\ker p=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta
.

Theorem 50
.

Let
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{H}}
be a representation on a complex vector space
{\mathcal{H}}
such that
\pi(E)\colon{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}
is injective and there exist a linear map
\varphi\colon{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
and an element
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
satisfying
\ker\varphi=\pi(E){\mathcal{H}}
and
\varphi(h)=1
. Define
\nu\in{\mathbb{C}}
by
\pi(H)\varphi=\nu\varphi
. Let
{\mathfrak{an}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\xi}}{{\curvearrowright}}V
be a real or complex representation such that
\xi(E)=0
and
\xi(H)=\nu+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
. Then we have the following “Hodge decomposition”:

\displaystyle C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=px+d\delta x+\delta dx,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p

\displaystyle=\ker(d\delta+\delta d)=\ker d\cap\ker\delta,

\displaystyle\ker p

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta,

\displaystyle\ker d

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d,

\displaystyle\ker\delta

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta.

Hence

H^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V)\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p.

9.3.2
Application to
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)

Let
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})
be the derivative of the discrete series representation. The operator
\pi(E)
is injective by (
6.3
) in Section
6.3
. By Proposition
22
, there exists a linear map
\varphi\colon C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\to{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\ker\varphi=\pi(E)C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})
and
\pi(H)\varphi=-\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}\varphi
. Hence we have
\nu=-\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}
.

Let
D_{n}^{\pm}=\bigoplus_{k=\pm n}^{\pm\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})_{(k)}
be the decomposition (
7
) in Section
5.1
. See also (
8
) in Section
5.2
. The subspace
(D_{n}^{\pm})_{(\pm k)}
is
1
-dimensional for all
k\geq n
. Since
(D_{n}^{\pm})_{(\pm k)}
is finite dimensional,
(D_{n}^{\pm})_{(\pm k)}\subset C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})
. See Corollary 4.4.3.3 of Warner
[
20
]
.

By the explicit formula of
\varphi
in Section 3.1 of Flaminio–Forni
[
6
]
, we know
\varphi\left((D_{n}^{\pm})_{(\pm n)}\right)\neq 0
. So we can take
h\in(D_{n}^{\pm})_{(\pm n)}
such that
\varphi(h)=1
.

Following the previous section, we define operators

T=\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\varphi h\right)

on
C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})
,

\delta=\iota(E)\otimes T\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

on
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)
and an element

h^{\prime}=\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\pi(H)h-\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}h\right)\in C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm}).

Lemma 51
.

h^{\prime}=\pm\sqrt{2}ih
.

Proof.

Recall the element
H_{0}=i\begin{pmatrix}&-\frac{1}{2}\\
\frac{1}{2}&\end{pmatrix}
, which acts on
(D_{n}^{\pm})_{(k)}
by multiplication by
k
. Let
H_{\pm}=i\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}&\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\end{pmatrix}\mp\begin{pmatrix}&\frac{1}{2}\\
\frac{1}{2}&\end{pmatrix}\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
. We have
\pi(H_{\mp})h=0
. See for example Section 2 of Chapter 8 of Taylor
[
18
]
. Writing

H_{\pm}=\sqrt{2}iH\mp 2E\pm iH_{0},

we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\pi(H_{\mp})h=\sqrt{2}i\pi(H)h\pm 2\pi(E)h-inh

(29)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}i\left(\pi(H)h-\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}h\right)\pm 2\pi(E)h.

Hence

h^{\prime}=\pi(E)^{-1}\left(\pm\sqrt{2}i\pi(E)h\right)=\pm\sqrt{2}ih.

∎

By Lemma
45
, we have

d\delta+\delta d=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-p,

where

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=\pm\sqrt{2}i\varepsilon(\theta_{H})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\varepsilon(\theta_{E})\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\left(\theta_{E}\pm\sqrt{2}i\theta_{H}\right)\iota(E)\otimes\varphi h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits.

Here
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)
is regarded as
\left(\bigwedge^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}}^{*}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\right)\right)\otimes C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}}
.

The subspace
\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p
is a graded subspace of
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)
. Let
\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{2}(\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p)^{i}
be the decomposition.

Lemma 52
.

We have

\displaystyle(\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p)^{0}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle(\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p)^{1}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{C}}\left(\theta_{E}\pm\sqrt{2}i\theta_{H}\right)\otimes h\otimes 1,

\displaystyle(\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p)^{2}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{C}}\left(\theta_{E}\wedge\theta_{H}\otimes h\otimes 1\right).

Proof.

For
f,g\in C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})
, we have

p\left(1\otimes f\otimes 1\right)=0,

p\left(\theta_{E}\otimes f\otimes 1+\theta_{H}\otimes g\otimes 1\right)=\varphi(f)\left(\theta_{E}\pm\sqrt{2}i\theta_{H}\right)\otimes h\otimes 1

and

p\left(\theta_{E}\wedge\theta_{H}\otimes f\otimes 1\right)=\varphi(f)\left(\theta_{E}\wedge\theta_{H}\otimes h\otimes 1\right),

from which the assertions follow.
∎

Theorem 53
.

We have

\displaystyle C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits\delta,

\displaystyle\ker d

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits d.

In particular,
H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{im}}\nolimits p
.

10
Computation results of
H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})

Here we state the computation results. Let
\Gamma
be a torsion free cocompact lattice of
\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
,
{\mathcal{F}}
be the orbit foliation of the action
\Gamma\backslash\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\curvearrowleft AN
by right multiplication and
g_{\Sigma}
be the genus of the surface
\Sigma=\Gamma\backslash\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})/\mathop{\mathrm{PSO}}\nolimits(2)
. Then we have

H^{i}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}})\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}&i=0\\
{\mathbb{C}}^{2g_{\Sigma}+1}&i=1\\
{\mathbb{C}}^{2g_{\Sigma}}&i=2\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

and

H^{i}\left({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}&i=1,2\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

For
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 2}
we have

H^{i}\left({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1-n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}^{2(2n-1)(g_{\Sigma}-1)}&i=1,2\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Or equivalently, for
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\leq-1}
,

H^{i}\left({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}^{2(1-2n)(g_{\Sigma}-1)}&i=1,2\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Let
\Delta_{\Sigma}
be the Laplacian of
\Sigma
, which is an operator on
L^{2}(\Sigma)
with domain
C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathbb{C}})
. Let
\sigma({\overline{\Delta_{\Sigma}}})
be the spectrum of the closure
{\overline{\Delta_{\Sigma}}}
of
\Delta_{\Sigma}
. We have
\sigma({\overline{\Delta_{\Sigma}}})\subset[0,\infty)
. For
\nu\in\sigma({\overline{\Delta_{\Sigma}}})
let
m_{\nu}
denote the multiplicity of the eigenvalue
\nu
. Then for
\nu\in\sigma({\overline{\Delta_{\Sigma}}})\setminus\{0\}
, we have

H^{i}\left({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1-4\nu}}{2\sqrt{2}}}\right)\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{\nu}}&i=1,2\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(30)

Set

\Lambda^{\prime}=\left\{\frac{n}{\sqrt{2}}\ \middle|\ n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\leq 1}\right\}\cup\left\{\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1-4\nu}}{2\sqrt{2}}\ \middle|\ \nu\in\sigma({\overline{\Delta_{\Sigma}}})\setminus\{0\}\right\}.

See Figure (
2
).

Figure 2:
\Lambda^{\prime}

Then

H^{*}\left({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)=0

for all
\lambda\not\in\Lambda^{\prime}
. Therefore,
H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})
is nontrivial if and only if
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\prime}
.

We have “Hodge decompositions” except for the case (
30
). The “Hodge decompositions” are given by Theorem
36
, Section
8
, Theorem
43
, Theorem
53
and Lemma
52
. For the case (
30
), the complex
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}\left({\mathcal{H}}_{-\frac{\nu}{2}}\right)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1-4\nu}}{2\sqrt{2}}}\right)
appears, for which we do not find a “Hodge decomposition”.

For
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
, computation results of
H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{R}}_{\lambda})
are obtained from those of
H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda})
and the isomorphism (
6
) in Section
2.2
.

11
Computation of the restriction map
H^{*}\left(\Gamma\backslash\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathbb{C}}\right)\to H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}})

The goal of this section is computation of the restriction map
H^{*}(P;{\mathbb{C}})\to H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}})
, which has an application as explained in Section
12
. The authors know two ways to compute this map. The first one is as follows. We compute
H^{*}(P;{\mathbb{C}})
by using the Serre spectral sequence of the circle bundle
P\to\Sigma
. Then we construct bases of
H^{*}(P;{\mathbb{C}})
and
H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}})
and compute how the restriction map maps the basis. This computation is relatively straightforward. We omit it in this paper. Instead, we will explain the second way of computation, which is less straightforward and is compatible with the way how we computed the cohomology
H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}})
.

The scheme of the computation is as follows. We use the isomorphism

H^{*}(P;{\mathbb{C}})\simeq H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\right).

We compute
H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)
for each irreducible unitary representation
{\mathcal{H}}
of
\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
by using the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence for the pair
{\mathfrak{an}}\subset\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and the computation results of
H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\lambda}\right)
for
\lambda=0
,
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
in Section
6.4
. We construct an operator
\delta
on
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\right)
of degree
-1
such that
d\delta+\delta d=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\Omega
, where
\Omega
is the Casimir element of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. This shows that a generic part of the cohomology
H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\right)
vanishes and only the part corresponding to
{\mathbb{C}}
and
C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})
in
C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})
can be nontrivial. So the computation results of
H^{*}(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathbb{C}})
and
H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)
conclude the computation of
H^{*}(P;{\mathbb{C}})
. The computation of the restriction map
H^{*}(P;{\mathbb{C}})\to H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}})
is based on the computation of the restriction maps

H^{*}(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathbb{C}})\to H^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})

and

H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)\to H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right).

Since we will show
H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)\to H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)
is an isomorphism, the noninjectivity and nonsurjectivity come only from
H^{*}(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathbb{C}})\to H^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})
.

11.1
Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence relative to a subalgebra

Since we will use the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence for the pair
{\mathfrak{an}}\subset\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, we explain here the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence relative to a subalgebra.

Let
K\subset L
be a field extension,
{\mathfrak{g}}
be a Lie algebra over
K
,
{\mathfrak{h}}
be a subalgebra of
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\pi\colon{\mathfrak{g}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits_{L}(V)
be a representation on a vector space
V
over
L
. Then we have a spectral sequence
E^{p,q}_{r}
defined from the filtered complex
F^{*}C^{*}({\mathfrak{g}};V)
, which converges to
H^{*}({\mathfrak{g}};V)
. See Section
6.1
. Recall that the differentials are
d\colon E^{p,q}_{r}\to E^{p+r,q-r+1}_{r}
.

Note that we have a representation
{\mathfrak{h}}\curvearrowright\bigwedge^{p}({\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{h}})^{*}\otimes V
defined from the adjoint representation and the restriction of
\pi
.

Proposition 54

(Hochschild–Serre
[
10
]
)
.

E^{p,q}_{1}\simeq H^{q}\left({\mathfrak{h}};\bigwedge^{p}({\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{h}})^{*}\otimes V\right).

Proof.

This is Corollary to Theorem 2 in
[
10
]
. Recall that

F^{p}C^{p+q}({\mathfrak{g}};V)=\left\{\varphi\in C^{p+q}({\mathfrak{g}};V)\ \middle|\ \iota_{X_{1}}\cdots\iota_{X_{q+1}}\varphi=0\ \text{if $X_{1},\ldots,X_{q+1}\in{\mathfrak{h}}$}\right\}.

Consider the map

\displaystyle F^{p}C^{p+q}({\mathfrak{g}};V)

\displaystyle\to C^{q}\left({\mathfrak{h}};\bigwedge^{p}({\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{h}})^{*}\otimes V\right)

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\mapsto((X_{1},\ldots,X_{q})\mapsto\iota_{X_{q}}\cdots\iota_{X_{1}}\varphi),

which induces

E^{p,q}_{0}=F^{p}C^{p+q}({\mathfrak{g}};V)/F^{p+1}C^{p+q}({\mathfrak{g}};V)\to C^{q}\left({\mathfrak{h}};\bigwedge^{p}({\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{h}})^{*}\otimes V\right).

This map gives an isomorphism

E^{p,q}_{0}\simeq C^{q}\left({\mathfrak{h}};\bigwedge^{p}({\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{h}})^{*}\otimes V\right)

of cochain complexes, hence the proposition follows by taking the cohomology. See
[
10
]
for the proof of the last sentence.
∎

11.2
Computation of
H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)
for an irreducible unitary representation
{\mathcal{H}}

Let
\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\curvearrowright{\mathcal{H}}
be an irreducible unitary representation and
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\curvearrowright C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
be the derivative representation. We compute
H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)
by using the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence.

Let
E_{r}^{p,q}
be the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence of
C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})
relative to the pair
{\mathfrak{an}}\subset\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, which converges to
H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)
. We have

\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})/{\mathfrak{an}}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}_{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}},\quad\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})/{\mathfrak{an}}\right)^{*}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}

as representations of
{\mathfrak{an}}
. By Proposition
54
,

\displaystyle E_{1}^{0,q}

\displaystyle\simeq H^{q}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right),

\displaystyle E_{1}^{1,q}

\displaystyle\simeq H^{q}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\otimes\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})/{\mathfrak{an}}\right)^{*}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq H^{q}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right).

Therefore, the
E_{1}
sheet is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right),}

where we have
d\colon E^{p,q}_{1}\to E^{p+1,q}_{1}
. We consider the spectral sequence separately depending on
{\mathcal{H}}
.

The trivial representation
{\mathbb{C}}

First we consider the case when
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathbb{C}}
. In this case the
E_{1}
sheet is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

by Section
6.4
. Since
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})=[\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}),\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})]
, we have
H^{1}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathbb{C}}\right)=0
directly from the definition. It follows that the only possibly nontrivial differential is actually nontrivial. Hence

H^{i}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathbb{C}}\right)\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}&i=0,3\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

The discrete series representations
D_{1}^{\pm}

Next we consider the case when
{\mathcal{H}}=D_{1}^{\pm}
. The
E_{1}
sheet is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

by Section
6.4
. So all the differentials of
E_{1}
are zero. Therefore,

H^{i}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}&i=1,2\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proposition 55
.

The homomorphism

H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)\to H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)

induced by the restriction map is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Let
F_{r}^{p,q}
be the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence of
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)
relative to the inclusion
{\mathfrak{an}}\subset{\mathfrak{an}}
. The restriction map

s\colon C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)\to C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)

is a cochain map and preserves the filtrations. Hence it induces a morphism
s\colon E_{r}^{p,q}\to F_{r}^{p,q}
of spectral sequences. We show
s\colon E_{1}\to F_{1}
is an isomorphism. The spectral sequence
F_{r}^{p,q}
converges to
H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)
and the
F_{1}
sheet is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

by Proposition
54
. Consider the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{1}^{0,q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}^{0,q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the horizontal arrows are those given by Proposition
54
. This is commutative by the description of the horizontal maps in the proof of Proposition
54
. Therefore,
s\colon E_{1}\to F_{1}
is an isomorphism. This implies that
s\colon E_{\infty}\to F_{\infty}
is also an isomorphism. Using the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{\infty}^{0,q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\infty}^{0,q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

we see
s\colon H^{q}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)\to H^{q}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)
is an isomorphism.
∎

The discrete series representations
D_{n}^{\pm}
for
n\geq 2

When
{\mathcal{H}}=D_{n}^{\pm}
for some
n\geq 2
, we have
E_{1}=0
by Section
6.4
. Hence

H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(D_{n}^{\pm})\right)=0.

The principal and complementary series representations
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mu}

When
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{\mu}
for some
\mu<0
, we have
E_{1}=0
by Section
6.4
. Hence

H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mu})\right)=0.

11.3
A construction of an operator
\delta
on
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)

In this section we will construct an operator
\delta
of degree
-1
on
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
such that

d\delta+\delta d=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\Omega\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\Omega,

where
{\mathcal{H}}
and
V
are representations of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and
\Omega
is the Casimir element of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. The existence of this operator virtually says that a generic part of the cohomology
H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
vanishes. This is an analogue of the operator
\delta
on
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
constructed in Section
7
and turns out to be an extension of an operator defined on a smaller subspace of
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
which appears in 2.3 of Borel–Wallach
[
4
]
. Though we consider
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
, we will need only the case when
V={\mathbb{R}}
for our application.

11.3.1
The definition of
\hat{\delta}
and computation of
\hat{d}\hat{\delta}+\hat{\delta}\hat{d}

Let
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{H}}
be a representation on a complex vector space and
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\xi}}{{\curvearrowright}}V
be a representation on a real vector space. Consider the tensor product
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\curvearrowright{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V
of the representations and the Chevalley–Eilenberg complex
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)=\bigwedge^{*}\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V
of it. We equip
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}
with the symmetric bilinear form defined by the duality between
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}
. (See Section
7.1
, where we defined a similar form for
{\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}
.) Let
C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)
be the Clifford algebra of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}
and
U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))
be the (real) universal enveloping algebra of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. Consider the tensor product

{\mathcal{A}}=C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)\otimes U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))\otimes U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))

of the algebras. Let
C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma}}{{\curvearrowright}}\bigwedge^{*}\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}
be the spin representation. Then the homomorphism

\sigma\colon C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\left(\bigwedge^{*}\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)

is an isomorphism of algebras. We have a representation

{\mathcal{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\xi}}{{\curvearrowright}}C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right).

Let
\hat{d}_{\star}\in C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)
be the unique element such that
\sigma(\hat{d}_{\star})
is the differential of the cochain complex
C^{*}(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathbb{R}})=\bigwedge^{*}\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}
. Recall the basis
E
,
H
,
F
of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and let
\theta_{E}
,
\theta_{H}
,
\theta_{F}\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}
be the dual basis of it. Define an element
\hat{d}\in{\mathcal{A}}
by

\displaystyle\hat{d}

\displaystyle=\hat{d}_{\star}\otimes 1\otimes 1+\theta_{E}\otimes E\otimes 1+\theta_{E}\otimes 1\otimes E

\displaystyle\quad+\theta_{H}\otimes H\otimes 1+\theta_{H}\otimes 1\otimes H

\displaystyle\quad+\theta_{F}\otimes F\otimes 1+\theta_{F}\otimes 1\otimes F.

Then
\left(\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\xi\right)(\hat{d})=d
, where
d
is the differential of
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
.

Now define
\hat{\delta}\in{\mathcal{A}}
by

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}

\displaystyle=E\otimes F\otimes 1+H\otimes H\otimes 1+F\otimes E\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad-E\otimes 1\otimes F-H\otimes 1\otimes H-F\otimes 1\otimes E

and put
\delta=\left(\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\xi\right)(\hat{\delta})
.

Theorem 56
.

We have

\hat{d}\hat{\delta}+\hat{\delta}\hat{d}=1\otimes\Omega\otimes 1-1\otimes 1\otimes\Omega

in
{\mathcal{A}}
, where
\Omega\in U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))
is the Casimir element of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
.

Proof.

In
C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)
we have

\displaystyle\hat{d}_{\star}X+X\hat{d}_{\star}=\hat{L}_{X},

\displaystyle\varphi X+X\varphi=\varphi(X)

for
X\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and
\varphi\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}
. Using these formulas and the definitions

\displaystyle\hat{d}

\displaystyle=\hat{d}_{\star}\otimes 1\otimes 1+\theta_{E}\otimes E\otimes 1+\theta_{E}\otimes 1\otimes E

\displaystyle\quad+\theta_{H}\otimes H\otimes 1+\theta_{H}\otimes 1\otimes H

\displaystyle\quad+\theta_{F}\otimes F\otimes 1+\theta_{F}\otimes 1\otimes F

and

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}

\displaystyle=E\otimes F\otimes 1+H\otimes H\otimes 1+F\otimes E\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad-E\otimes 1\otimes F-H\otimes 1\otimes H-F\otimes 1\otimes E,

we get

\displaystyle\hat{d}\hat{\delta}+\hat{\delta}\hat{d}

\displaystyle=\hat{L}_{E}\otimes F\otimes 1+\hat{L}_{H}\otimes H\otimes 1+\hat{L}_{F}\otimes E\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad-\hat{L}_{E}\otimes 1\otimes F-\hat{L}_{H}\otimes 1\otimes H-\hat{L}_{F}\otimes 1\otimes E

\displaystyle\quad+\theta_{E}E\otimes EF\otimes 1+(1-\theta_{E}E)\otimes FE\otimes 1+\theta_{E}H\otimes EH\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad-\theta_{E}H\otimes HE\otimes 1-1\otimes E\otimes F

\displaystyle\quad+1\otimes F\otimes E-\theta_{E}E\otimes 1\otimes EF-(1-\theta_{E}E)\otimes 1\otimes FE

\displaystyle\quad-\theta_{E}H\otimes 1\otimes EH+\theta_{E}H\otimes 1\otimes HE

\displaystyle\quad+\theta_{H}E\otimes HF\otimes 1-\theta_{H}E\otimes FH\otimes 1+1\otimes H^{2}\otimes 1+\theta_{H}F\otimes HE\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad-\theta_{H}F\otimes EH\otimes 1-1\otimes H\otimes H

\displaystyle\quad+1\otimes H\otimes H-\theta_{H}E\otimes 1\otimes HF+\theta_{H}E\otimes 1\otimes FH-1\otimes 1\otimes H^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\theta_{H}F\otimes 1\otimes HE+\theta_{H}F\otimes 1\otimes EH

\displaystyle\quad+\theta_{F}H\otimes FH\otimes 1-\theta_{F}H\otimes HF\otimes 1+\theta_{F}F\otimes FE\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad+(1-\theta_{F}F)\otimes EF\otimes 1-1\otimes F\otimes E

\displaystyle\quad+1\otimes E\otimes F-\theta_{F}H\otimes 1\otimes FH+\theta_{F}H\otimes 1\otimes HF-\theta_{F}F\otimes 1\otimes FE

\displaystyle\quad-(1-\theta_{F}F)\otimes 1\otimes EF.

By the formula

\hat{L}_{X}=-\theta_{E}[X,E]-\theta_{H}[X,H]-\theta_{F}[X,F]

for
X\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{E}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{H}E-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{F}H,\quad\hat{L}_{H}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{E}E+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{F}F,

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{F}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{E}H-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{H}F.

Recalling

\Omega=FE+H^{2}+EF,

it follows that

\displaystyle\hat{d}\hat{\delta}+\hat{\delta}\hat{d}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{H}E-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{F}H\right)\otimes F\otimes 1+\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{E}E+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{F}F\right)\otimes H\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{E}H-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{H}F\right)\otimes E\otimes 1-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{H}E-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{F}H\right)\otimes 1\otimes F

\displaystyle\quad-\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{E}E+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{F}F\right)\otimes 1\otimes H-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{E}H-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\theta_{H}F\right)\otimes 1\otimes E

\displaystyle\quad+1\otimes FE\otimes 1-1\otimes E\otimes F+1\otimes F\otimes E-1\otimes 1\otimes FE+1\otimes H^{2}\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad-1\otimes H\otimes H+1\otimes H\otimes H-1\otimes 1\otimes H^{2}+1\otimes EF\otimes 1-1\otimes F\otimes E

\displaystyle\quad+1\otimes E\otimes F-1\otimes 1\otimes EF

\displaystyle\quad+\theta_{E}E\otimes\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H\otimes 1+\theta_{E}H\otimes\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}E\right)\otimes 1-\theta_{E}E\otimes 1\otimes\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H

\displaystyle\quad+\theta_{E}H\otimes 1\otimes\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}E+\theta_{H}E\otimes\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}F\right)\otimes 1+\theta_{H}F\otimes\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}E\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad+\theta_{H}E\otimes 1\otimes\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}F-\theta_{H}F\otimes 1\otimes\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}E+\theta_{F}H\otimes\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}F\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad+\theta_{F}F\otimes\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H\right)\otimes 1+\theta_{F}H\otimes 1\otimes\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}F\right)-\theta_{F}F\otimes 1\otimes\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H\right)

\displaystyle=1\otimes FE\otimes 1-1\otimes 1\otimes FE+1\otimes H^{2}\otimes 1-1\otimes 1\otimes H^{2}+1\otimes EF\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad-1\otimes 1\otimes EF

\displaystyle=1\otimes\Omega\otimes 1-1\otimes 1\otimes\Omega.

∎

Corollary 57
.

We have

d\delta+\delta d=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\pi(\Omega)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(\Omega)

on
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
.

Remark 58
.

We can get

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(EH\otimes(F\otimes 1+1\otimes F)+HF\otimes(E\otimes 1+1\otimes E)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\quad+FE\otimes(H\otimes 1+1\otimes H)\right),

which shows
\delta^{2}
is not necessarily
0
on
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
.

11.3.2
The operator
\delta
preserves
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}),\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2);{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)

Now we will prove that the operator
\delta
preserves the subcomplex

C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}),\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2);{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)

of
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
, which will be defined later. Since we will not use this property in this paper, this section and Section
11.3.3
can be skipped.

First we will define a representation
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{A}}
. Recall that we have the adjoint representation
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits}}{{\curvearrowright}}U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))
defined by

(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits X)u=Xu-uX

for
X\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and
u\in U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))
. We also have a representation
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits}}{{\curvearrowright}}C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)
defined by

(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits X)x=\hat{L}_{X}x-x\hat{L}_{X}

for
X\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and
x\in C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)
. This is actually a representation since

•

the map

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})

\displaystyle\to C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto\hat{L}_{X}

is a Lie algebra homomorphism, ie the formula

\hat{L}_{[X,Y]}=\hat{L}_{X}\hat{L}_{Y}-\hat{L}_{Y}\hat{L}_{X}

holds for all
X
,
Y\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
,

•

the map

\displaystyle C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)

\displaystyle\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\left(C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)\right)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(y\mapsto xy-yx\right)

is the adjoint representation of the Lie algebra
C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)

•

and it is the composition of these two Lie algebra homomorphisms.

The notation
\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits
for the representation
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\curvearrowright C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)
is justified by the following lemma.

Lemma 59
.

We have

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{X}Y-Y\hat{L}_{X}=[X,Y],\quad\hat{L}_{X}\varphi-\varphi\hat{L}_{X}=-\varphi\circ\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits X

in
C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)
for
X
,
Y\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and
\varphi\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}
. Here
[X,Y]
in the first equation is the Lie bracket of
X
and
Y
in the Lie algebra
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, not in
C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)
.

Proof.

Let
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
,
X_{3}
be a basis of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}
,
\theta_{3}\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}
be its dual basis. The lemma follows from the computation based on the formula

\hat{L}_{X}=-\sum_{i}\theta_{i}[X,X_{i}].

∎

Consider the direct product
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\curvearrowright\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}
of the adjoint representation
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\curvearrowright\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and its dual
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\curvearrowright\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}
. Note that we have a natural inclusion
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\subset C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)
. The representation
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits}}{{\curvearrowright}}C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)
is obtained by extending
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\curvearrowright\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}
to
C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)
so that all the operators act as derivations of the algebra
C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)
.

By taking tensor product of representations, we get a representation

\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{A}}.

Consider the Lie subalgebra
\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2)
of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. Let
R=E-F\in\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2)
. Then
R
is a basis of
\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2)
.

Proposition 60
.

We have

\hat{\delta}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2)},

ie
(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits R)\hat{\delta}=0
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits R)E=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H,\quad(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits R)H=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(E+F),\quad(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits R)F=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H.

Using the definition

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}

\displaystyle=E\otimes F\otimes 1+H\otimes H\otimes 1+F\otimes E\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad-E\otimes 1\otimes F-H\otimes 1\otimes H-F\otimes 1\otimes E,

we get

\displaystyle(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits R)\hat{\delta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H\otimes F\otimes 1+E\otimes\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}F\right)\otimes 1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(E+F)\otimes H\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad+H\otimes\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(E+F)\right)\otimes 1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H\otimes E\otimes 1+F\otimes\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H\otimes 1\otimes F-E\otimes 1\otimes\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(E+F)\otimes 1\otimes H

\displaystyle\quad-H\otimes 1\otimes\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(E+F)\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H\otimes 1\otimes E-F\otimes 1\otimes\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H

\displaystyle=0.

∎

Lemma 61
.

We have

\hat{\delta}\left(R\otimes 1\otimes 1\right)+\left(R\otimes 1\otimes 1\right)\hat{\delta}=0

in
{\mathcal{A}}
.

Proof.

This follows from the equation
RX+XR=0
in
C\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}\right)
for any
X\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
.
∎

We define a representation
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}}}}{{\curvearrowright}}C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
by

\displaystyle({\mathcal{L}}_{X}\varphi)\left(X_{1},\ldots,X_{p}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{p}\varphi\left(X_{1},\ldots,[X,X_{i}],\ldots,X_{p}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\pi(X)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(X)\right)\varphi(X_{1},\ldots,X_{p})

for
\varphi\in C^{p}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
and
X
,
X_{1},\ldots,X_{p}\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. Then we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{X}

\displaystyle=L_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\pi(X)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(X)

\displaystyle=\left(\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\xi\right)\left(\hat{L}_{X}\otimes 1\otimes 1+1\otimes X\otimes 1+1\otimes 1\otimes X\right)

for
X\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
.

Define
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}),\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2);{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)\subset C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
by

C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}),\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2);{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)=\left\{\varphi\in C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)\ \middle|\ \begin{gathered}{\mathcal{L}}_{R}\varphi=0,\\
\left(\iota(R)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)\varphi=0\end{gathered}\right\}.

Since

\displaystyle d{\mathcal{L}}_{R}={\mathcal{L}}_{R}d,\quad d\left(\iota(R)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)+\left(\iota(R)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)d={\mathcal{L}}_{R},

the subspace
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}),\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2);{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
is a subcomplex of
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
.

By the definition of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{A}}
, we have

\displaystyle(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits X)x

\displaystyle=\left(\hat{L}_{X}\otimes 1\otimes 1+1\otimes X\otimes 1+1\otimes 1\otimes X\right)x

\displaystyle\quad-x\left(\hat{L}_{X}\otimes 1\otimes 1+1\otimes X\otimes 1+1\otimes 1\otimes X\right)

for any
X\in\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and
x\in{\mathcal{A}}
, hence

\left(\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\xi\right)\left((\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits X)x\right)={\mathcal{L}}_{X}\left(\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\xi\right)(x)-\left(\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\xi\right)(x){\mathcal{L}}_{X},

(31)

which means

\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\xi\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits\left(C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)\right)

is
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
-equivariant.

Proposition 62
.

The operator
\delta
preserves
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}),\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2);{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
.

Proof.

Take
\varphi\in C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}),\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2);{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
. By Equation (
31
) and Proposition
60
, we have

0=\left(\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\xi\right)\left((\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits R)\hat{\delta}\right)={\mathcal{L}}_{R}\delta-\delta{\mathcal{L}}_{R}.

Thus
{\mathcal{L}}_{R}\delta\varphi=0
. Applying
\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\xi
to the equation in Lemma
61
, we also get

\delta\left(\iota(R)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)+\left(\iota(R)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)\delta=0,

hence
\left(\iota(R)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)\delta\varphi=0
. Therefore
\delta\varphi\in C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}),\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2);{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
.
∎

11.3.3
Relation with the usual codifferential by Hodge theory

In this section we will show that the operator
\delta
on
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
is an extension of an operator
\delta_{MM}
considered in 2.3 of Borel–Wallach
[
4
]
defined on certain subspace of
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
which contains
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}),\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2);{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
. The operator
\delta_{MM}
is a generalization of (an extension of) an operator considered in Matsushima–Murakami
[
15
]
.

Let
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})=\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2)\oplus{\mathfrak{p}}
be the Cartan decomposition with respect to the Cartan involution
\theta\colon X\mapsto-X^{\top}
. Consider a graded vector space

D^{*}=\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V.

The projection
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\to{\mathfrak{p}}
induces a map
\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}\to\bigwedge^{*}\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})^{*}
and hence an injective map

i\colon D^{*}\to C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right).

By identifying the image of
i
, we get

D^{*}\simeq\left\{\varphi\in C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)\ \middle|\ \left(\iota(R)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)\varphi=0\right\}.

(32)

Note that, by recalling the formula

d\left(\iota(R)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)+\left(\iota(R)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\right)d={\mathcal{L}}_{R},

the right hand side of (
32
) is not a subcomplex of
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
in general.

By using representations
\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathfrak{p}}
,
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{H}}
and
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\xi}}{{\curvearrowright}}V
, we get a representation
\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}}}}{{\curvearrowright}}D^{*}
. Define a graded subspace
C^{*}\subset D^{*}
by

C^{*}=\left(D^{*}\right)^{\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2)}.

Since
i\colon D^{*}\to C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
is
\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2)
-equivariant with respect to
{\mathcal{L}}
and
{\mathcal{L}}|_{\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2)}
, we obtain a natural isomorphism

C^{*}\simeq C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}),\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2);{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right).

Let
C\left({\mathfrak{p}}\oplus{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}\right)
be the Clifford algebra of
{\mathfrak{p}}\oplus{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}
equipped with the symmetric bilinear form defined by the duality of
{\mathfrak{p}}
and
{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}
. Let

{\mathcal{B}}=C\left({\mathfrak{p}}\oplus{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}\right)\otimes U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))\otimes U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})).

We have a representation
{\mathcal{B}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\xi}}{{\curvearrowright}}D^{*}
, where
C\left({\mathfrak{p}}\oplus{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma}}{{\curvearrowright}}\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{p}}^{*}
is the spin representation. Therefore we have the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}),\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2);{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\curvearrowleft}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\curvearrowleft}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}

Note that
H,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(E+F)\in{\mathfrak{p}}
is an orthonormal basis of
{\mathfrak{p}}
with respect to
B
. See Section
3
. Define
\hat{\delta}_{MM}\in{\mathcal{B}}
by

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}_{MM}

\displaystyle=H\otimes H\otimes 1+\frac{1}{2}(E+F)\otimes(E+F)\otimes 1

\displaystyle\quad-H\otimes 1\otimes H-\frac{1}{2}(E+F)\otimes 1\otimes(E+F)

and set
\delta_{MM}=\left(\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\xi\right)(\hat{\delta}_{MM})
. Then
\delta_{MM}
is an operator on
D^{*}
of degree
-1
. The operator
\delta_{MM}
is the same as the operator in 2.3 of Borel–Wallach
[
4
]
.

The following proposition describes the relation between
\delta
and
\delta_{MM}
, where
\delta
is the operator constructed in the previous section.

Proposition 63
.

We have

\delta|_{D^{*}}=\delta_{MM}

by identifying
D^{*}
with a subspace of
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
through the isomorphism (
32
).

Proof.

Following the decomposition
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})=\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2)\oplus{\mathfrak{p}}
, we get

\displaystyle E=\frac{1}{2}R+\frac{1}{2}(E+F),\quad F=-\frac{1}{2}R+\frac{1}{2}(E+F).

On
D^{*}
we have

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle=\iota(E)\otimes\pi(F)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\iota(H)\otimes\pi(H)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\iota(F)\otimes\pi(E)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle\quad-\iota(E)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(F)-\iota(H)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(H)-\iota(F)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(E)

\displaystyle=\iota\left(\frac{1}{2}(E+F)\right)\otimes\pi(F)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\iota(H)\otimes\pi(H)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\iota\left(\frac{1}{2}(E+F)\right)\otimes\pi(E)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle\quad-\iota\left(\frac{1}{2}(E+F)\right)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(F)-\iota(H)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(H)-\iota\left(\frac{1}{2}(E+F)\right)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(E)

\displaystyle=\iota(H)\otimes\pi(H)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits+\iota\left(\frac{1}{2}(E+F)\right)\otimes\pi(E+F)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

\displaystyle\quad-\iota(H)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(H)-\iota\left(\frac{1}{2}(E+F)\right)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\xi(E+F)

\displaystyle=\delta_{MM}.

∎

Corollary 64
.

The operator
\delta_{MM}
preserves
C^{*}
.

Proof.

This is because
\delta|_{D^{*}}=\delta_{MM}
and
\delta
preserves
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}),\mathop{\mathfrak{so}}\nolimits(2);{\mathcal{H}}\otimes V\right)
.
∎

11.4
Computation of
H^{*}\left(\Gamma\backslash\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathbb{C}}\right)

In this section we compute the de Rham cohomology
H^{*}(P;{\mathbb{C}})
, where
P=\Gamma\backslash\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and
\Gamma
is a torsion free cocompact lattice of
\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. (As we said before this cohomology can be computed by using the Serre spectral sequence of the circle bundle
P\to\Sigma
, but here we will compute it by using the representation theory.)

By Proposition
3
there is an isomorphism

\Gamma\left(\bigwedge^{*}T^{*}P\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\right)\simeq C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\right)

of cochain complexes. Let

L^{2}(P)=W_{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{\mu\neq 0}W_{\mu}

be the decomposition (
9
), where
W_{\mu}
is the sum of the irreducible unitary subrepresentations of
L^{2}(P)
with Casimir parameter
\mu
. This is a decomposition of a unitary representation of
\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. By Proposition
5
and Lemma
35
, we obtain

C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})=C^{\infty}(W_{0})\oplus C^{\infty}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu\neq 0}W_{\mu}\right),

which is a decomposition of a representation of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. Therefore

C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\right)\simeq C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(W_{0})\right)\oplus C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu\neq 0}W_{\mu}\right)\right)

as cochain complexes.

Put
{\mathcal{H}}=C^{\infty}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu\neq 0}W_{\mu}\right)
. Applying Corollary
57
to
{\mathcal{H}}
and
V={\mathbb{R}}
, there exists an operator
\delta
on
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\right)
such that

d\delta+\delta d=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\pi(\Omega),

where
\pi
is the representation of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
on
{\mathcal{H}}
. We can show that
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\pi(\Omega)
is bijective on
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\right)
by following the proof of Lemma
34
. Putting
\delta^{\prime}=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\pi(\Omega))^{-1}\delta
, we have
d\delta^{\prime}+\delta^{\prime}d=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
on
C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\right)
. Therefore

H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathcal{H}}\right)=0.

It follows that

H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\right)\simeq H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(W_{0})\right).

(33)

By Section
5.3.4
we have
W_{0}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}\oplus g_{\Sigma}(D_{1}^{+}\oplus D_{1}^{-})
as unitary representations of
\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
, where
g_{\Sigma}
is the genus of the surface
\Sigma=P/\mathop{\mathrm{PSO}}\nolimits(2)
. Hence

C^{\infty}(W_{0})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}\oplus g_{\Sigma}\left(C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{+})\oplus C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{-})\right)

by Lemma
35
and

\displaystyle C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(W_{0})\right)\simeq C^{*}(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathbb{C}})

\displaystyle\oplus g_{\Sigma}C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{+})\right)

\displaystyle\oplus g_{\Sigma}C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{-})\right).

By Section
11.2
we get the following.

Proposition 65
.

\displaystyle H^{*}(P;{\mathbb{C}})

\displaystyle\simeq H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}&i=0,3\\
{\mathbb{C}}^{2g_{\Sigma}}&i=1,2\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

11.5
The restriction map
H^{*}\left(\Gamma\backslash\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathbb{C}}\right)\to H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}})

In this section we compute the restriction map
H^{*}(P;{\mathbb{C}})\to H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}})
. This is the goal of Section
11
and will be applied in Section
12
.

Consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\left(\bigwedge^{*}T^{*}P\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(W_{0})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\left(\bigwedge^{*}T^{*}{\mathcal{F}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(W_{0})\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

of cochain complexes and cochain maps, where the vertical maps are given by restriction. By taking cohomology and applying Theorem
36
and (
33
), we get

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}(P;{\mathbb{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(W_{0})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(W_{0})\right).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Fix an isomorphism
C^{\infty}(W_{0})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}\oplus g_{\Sigma}C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{+})\oplus g_{\Sigma}C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{-})
of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
-modules. Then we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(W_{0})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathbb{C}})\oplus\bigoplus_{\epsilon=\pm}g_{\Sigma}H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\epsilon})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(W_{0})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}})\oplus\bigoplus_{\epsilon=\pm}g_{\Sigma}H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\epsilon})\right).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

By Proposition
55
, the restriction map
H^{*}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)\to H^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(D_{1}^{\pm})\right)
is an isomorphism.

By Section
11.2
and
6.4
, we have

H^{i}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathbb{C}}\right)\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}&i=0,3\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

and

H^{i}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}\right)\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}&i=0,1\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

It follows that
H^{i}\left(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}});{\mathbb{C}}\right)\to H^{i}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}\right)
is the identity map when
i=0,2
and is
0
otherwise.

Theorem 66
.

The homomorphism
H^{i}(P;{\mathbb{C}})\to H^{i}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}})
induced by the restriction map is an isomorphism when
i=0,2
.

12
Application:
{\mathcal{F}}
is totally minimizable

In this section we prove that the foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
is totally minimizable.

Definition 67
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a
C^{\infty}
foliation of a
C^{\infty}
manifold
M
. Following Matsumoto
[
13
]
, we say that
{\mathcal{G}}
is totally minimizable if for any
C^{\infty}
Riemannian metric
g
of
T{\mathcal{G}}
, there exists a
C^{\infty}
Riemannian metric
\tilde{g}
on
M
such that
\tilde{g}|_{T{\mathcal{G}}\times_{M}T{\mathcal{G}}}=g
and every leaf of
{\mathcal{G}}
is a minimal submanifold of the Riemannian manifold
(M,\tilde{g})
.

Let
\Gamma
be a torsion free cocompact lattice of
\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. Put
P=\Gamma\backslash\mathop{\mathrm{PSL}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
and let
{\mathcal{F}}
be the orbit foliation of the action of
AN
on
P
by right multiplication. By identifying
P
with the unit tangent bundle of the hyperbolic surface
\Sigma=P/\mathop{\mathrm{PSO}}\nolimits(2)
, the foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
is identified with the weak stable foliation of the geodesic flow of
\Sigma
.

It seems that in Haefliger
[
7
]
and Haefliger–Li
[
8
]
, Haefliger and Li were interested in the following problem.

Problem 68
.

Is
{\mathcal{F}}
totally minimizable?

Haefliger obtained the following weaker result.

Proposition 69

(Haefliger
[
7
]
)
.

For any
C^{\infty}
Riemannian metric
g
of
T{\mathcal{F}}
and any open neighborhood
U
of
g
in
\Gamma\left(\bigwedge^{2}T^{*}{\mathcal{F}}\right)
with the
C^{\infty}
topology, there exists a
C^{\infty}
Riemannian metric
\tilde{g}
on
P
such that
\tilde{g}|_{T{\mathcal{F}}\times_{P}T{\mathcal{F}}}\in U
and every leaf of
{\mathcal{F}}
is a minimal submanifold of
(P,\tilde{g})
.

This proposition follows from Corollary 5 of Theorem 4.1 in
[
7
]
and the classification of holonomy invariant
1
-currents of
{\mathcal{F}}
on page 281 of
[
7
]
.

We will prove the following theorem.

Theorem 70
.

The foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
of
P
is totally minimizable.

The approach by Haefliger and Li to Problem
68
and our solution are both based on the following theorem.

Theorem 71

(Rummler–Sullivan
[
16
,
17
]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a
p
-dimensional oriented
C^{\infty}
foliation of a
C^{\infty}
manifold
M
and
g
be a
C^{\infty}
Riemannian metric of
T{\mathcal{G}}
. Let
\omega\in\Gamma\left(\bigwedge^{p}T^{*}{\mathcal{G}}\right)
be the volume form of
g
. Then the following two conditions are equivalent:

1.

There exists a
C^{\infty}
Riemannian metric
\tilde{g}
on
M
such that
\tilde{g}|_{T{\mathcal{G}}\times_{M}T{\mathcal{G}}}=g
and every leaf of
{\mathcal{G}}
is a minimal submanifold of
(M,\tilde{g})

2.

There exists a
C^{\infty}

p
-form
\tilde{\omega}
on
M
such that
\tilde{\omega}|_{T{\mathcal{G}}\times_{M}\cdots\times_{M}T{\mathcal{G}}}=\omega
and

d\tilde{\omega}\left(X_{1},\ldots,X_{p+1}\right)=0

for
X_{1},\ldots,X_{p}\in\Gamma(T{\mathcal{G}})
and
X_{p+1}\in\Gamma(TM)
.

A short proof of this theorem can be found in Haefliger
[
7
]
.

Though we use the same theorem as Haefliger and Li, our approach is different from theirs. To consider whether
\omega
has an extension
\tilde{\omega}
satisfying the second condition of Theorem
71
, Haefliger and Li translate the problem into a problem of certain
1
-form
d\int_{\mathcal{F}}\omega
on a complete transversal of the foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
. Instead of dealing with objects on a transversal, we consider the problem directly by dealing with leafwise objects.

Corollary 72

(Matsumoto
[
13
]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a
p
-dimensional oriented
C^{\infty}
foliation of a
C^{\infty}
manifold
M
. If the restriction map

H^{p}(M;{\mathbb{R}})\to H^{p}({\mathcal{G}};{\mathbb{R}})

is surjective, the foliation
{\mathcal{G}}
is totally minimizable.

Proof.

The proof is taken from
[
13
]
for the convenience of the reader. Let
r
denote the restriction maps. Take any
C^{\infty}
Riemannian metric
g
of
T{\mathcal{G}}
and let
\omega\in\Gamma\left(\bigwedge^{p}T^{*}{\mathcal{G}}\right)
be the volume form of
g
. We have
[\omega]\in H^{p}({\mathcal{G}};{\mathbb{R}})
. By assumption, there exists
[\omega^{\prime}]\in H^{p}(M;{\mathbb{R}})
such that
r[\omega^{\prime}]=[\omega]
. So we have
r\omega^{\prime}=\omega+d_{\mathcal{G}}\eta
for some
\eta\in\Gamma\left(\bigwedge^{p-1}T^{*}{\mathcal{G}}\right)
. We can take
\eta^{\prime}\in\Gamma\left(\bigwedge^{p-1}T^{*}M\right)
such that
r\eta^{\prime}=\eta
. Define
\tilde{\omega}\in\Gamma(\bigwedge^{p}T^{*}M)
by
\tilde{\omega}=\omega^{\prime}-d\eta^{\prime}
. Then

r\tilde{\omega}=\omega+d_{\mathcal{G}}\eta-d_{\mathcal{G}}r\eta^{\prime}=\omega

and
d\tilde{\omega}=0
. Hence
\tilde{\omega}
satisfies the second condition of Theorem
71
.
∎

So Theorem
70
follows from the next lemma.

Lemma 73
.

The restriction map

H^{2}(P;{\mathbb{R}})\to H^{2}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{R}})

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\left(\bigwedge^{*}T^{*}P\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\left(\bigwedge^{*}T^{*}{\mathcal{F}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\left(\bigwedge^{*}T^{*}P\right)\oplus i\Gamma\left(\bigwedge^{*}T^{*}P\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\left(\bigwedge^{*}T^{*}{\mathcal{F}}\right)\oplus i\Gamma\left(\bigwedge^{*}T^{*}{\mathcal{F}}\right)}

of real cochain complexes, where the horizontal maps are given by restriction. It induces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}(P;{\mathbb{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}(P;{\mathbb{R}})\oplus iH^{*}(P;{\mathbb{R}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{R}})\oplus iH^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{R}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}(P;{\mathbb{R}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{R}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}

So the lemma follows from Theorem
66
.
∎

13
Computation of
H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathfrak{an}})
by a Hodge decomposition

We will compute the de Rham cohomology
H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathfrak{an}})
for the adjoint representation
{\mathfrak{an}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathfrak{an}}
by giving a “Hodge decomposition” of
C^{*}({\mathfrak{an}};C^{\infty}(P,{\mathbb{C}})\otimes{\mathfrak{an}})
. Since
{\mathfrak{n}}
acts nontrivially on
{\mathfrak{an}}
, we cannot apply the results in Section
7.1
.

However, if we want only the computation of
H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathfrak{an}})
, we can compute it as follows. In this case we do not obtain a Hodge decomposition.

There is a short exact sequence

0\to{\mathfrak{n}}\to{\mathfrak{an}}\to{\mathfrak{an}}/{\mathfrak{n}}\to 0

of
{\mathfrak{an}}
-modules. This is isomorphic to

0\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\to{\mathfrak{an}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{0}\to 0,

which, together with the computation results of
H^{*}\left({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{R}}_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\right)
and
H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{R}}_{0})
in Section
10
, gives a long exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathfrak{an}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}}^{2g_{\Sigma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathfrak{an}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}}^{2g_{\Sigma}+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathfrak{an}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}}.}

It is easy to show
H^{0}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathfrak{an}})=0
. In
[
3
]
, Asaoka showed
H^{1}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathfrak{an}})\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{2g_{\Sigma}}
by using a result in Matsumoto–Mitsumatsu
[
14
]
. Therefore we get
H^{2}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathfrak{an}})\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{2g_{\Sigma}}
from the long exact sequence.

13.1
Hodge decomposition of
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathfrak{an}}\right)
for generic
{\mathcal{H}}

Let
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathcal{H}}
be a representation on a complex vector space
{\mathcal{H}}
. Consider the tensor product
{\mathfrak{an}}\curvearrowright{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathfrak{an}}
of the restriction
{\mathfrak{an}}\curvearrowright{\mathcal{H}}
and the adjoint representation
{\mathfrak{an}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits}}{{\curvearrowright}}{\mathfrak{an}}
. In this section we will construct an operator
\delta
on
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathfrak{an}}\right)
of degree
-1
such that

d\delta+\delta d=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\pi(\Omega)^{2}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,

where
\Omega
is the Casimir element of
\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}})
.

Let
\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits\colon U({\mathfrak{an}})\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{an}})
be the extension of the adjoint representation
\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits\colon{\mathfrak{an}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{an}})
and
I
be the kernel of
\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits\colon U({\mathfrak{an}})\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{an}})
. Consider the algebra

{\mathcal{A}}=C({\mathfrak{an}}\oplus{\mathfrak{an}}^{*})\otimes U(\mathop{\mathfrak{sl}}\nolimits(2,{\mathbb{R}}))\otimes U({\mathfrak{an}})/I,

which has a natural representation

{\mathcal{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits}}{{\curvearrowright}}C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathfrak{an}}\right)=\bigwedge^{*}{\mathfrak{an}}^{*}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathfrak{an}}.

Define an element
\hat{d}\in{\mathcal{A}}
by

\hat{d}=\hat{d}_{\star}\otimes 1\otimes 1+\theta_{H}\otimes H\otimes 1+\theta_{H}\otimes 1\otimes H+\theta_{E}\otimes E\otimes 1+\theta_{E}\otimes 1\otimes E,

which satisfies
\left(\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits\right)(\hat{d})=d
for the differential
d
. Define
\hat{\delta}\in{\mathcal{A}}
by

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}

\displaystyle=\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}\otimes\Omega\otimes 1-6\hat{d}_{\star}^{\top}\otimes F\otimes E+H\otimes H\Omega\otimes 1-\sqrt{2}H\otimes F\otimes E

\displaystyle\quad-2H\otimes FH\otimes E+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H\otimes\Omega\otimes 1-H\otimes\Omega\otimes H

\displaystyle\quad+2E\otimes F\Omega\otimes 1-4E\otimes F^{2}\otimes E

and put
\delta=\left(\sigma\otimes\pi\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits\right)(\hat{\delta})
, which is an operator of degree
-1
on
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathfrak{an}}\right)
.

Lemma 74
.

We have

H^{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}H,\quad E^{2}=0,\quad HE=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}E,\quad EH=0

in
U({\mathfrak{an}})/I
.

Proof.

The formulas follow from easy computation.
∎

Theorem 75
.

We have

\hat{d}\hat{\delta}+\hat{\delta}\hat{d}=1\otimes\Omega^{2}\otimes 1

in
{\mathcal{A}}
.

Proof.

A proof can be given by a similar computation as in the proof of Theorems
29
and
56
using Lemma
74
. We omit the proof.
∎

Corollary 76
.

We have

d\delta+\delta d=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\pi(\Omega)^{2}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

on
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathfrak{an}}\right)
.

Proposition 77
.

We have
\hat{\delta}^{2}=0
in
{\mathcal{A}}
.

Proof.

This follows from a direct computation as in the proof of Proposition
32
using Lemma
74
. We omit the proof.
∎

13.2
Hodge decomposition of
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}\otimes{\mathfrak{an}}\right)

We take the following elements as a basis of
C^{*}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}\otimes{\mathfrak{an}}\right)
:

\displaystyle 1\otimes H,\ \ \ 1\otimes E,

\displaystyle\theta_{H}\otimes H,\ \ \ \theta_{H}\otimes E,\ \ \ \theta_{E}\otimes H,\ \ \ \theta_{E}\otimes E,

\displaystyle\theta_{H}\wedge\theta_{E}\otimes H,\ \ \ \theta_{H}\wedge\theta_{E}\otimes E.

Let
d_{i}\colon C^{i}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}\otimes{\mathfrak{an}}\right)\to C^{i+1}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}\otimes{\mathfrak{an}}\right)
be the differential. Then we have

d_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\\
0&0\\
-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&0\end{pmatrix},\quad d_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&0\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}

with respect to the basis.

Let
\delta_{i}\colon C^{i}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}\otimes{\mathfrak{an}}\right)\to C^{i-1}\left({\mathfrak{an}};{\mathbb{C}}\otimes{\mathfrak{an}}\right)
be a linear map. Then

d\delta+\delta d=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

if and only if

\delta_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}a&0&b&-\sqrt{2}\\
c&\sqrt{2}&e&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\delta_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\sqrt{2}\\
e&-c\\
-\sqrt{2}&0\\
-b&a\end{pmatrix}

for some
a
,
b
,
c
,
e\in{\mathbb{C}}
. This
\delta
satisfies
\delta^{2}=0
. Therefore
